Report failed grasps, invalid subgoals, contact mistakes, transfer to new rooms or objects, recovery after bumps, and logs of actual state changes instead of only the written plan.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the abstract state leaves out contact, timing, weight, reach, or friction, when subgoal labels hide impossible actions, or when benchmarks grade descriptions instead of execution.

Connection

PACT makes allowed movement explicit. ReaSyn treats molecule building as a route that must be possible. Skill-Pro stores reusable procedures. Embodied models bring the same idea into the physical world: intelligence has to move through states the world permits.

EXEMPLAR 26

Protein Autoregressive Modeling And DeCoDe

Biological Generation As Respecting How Proteins Work

Problem

A protein is not useful just because its amino-acid string looks common. The string has to fold into a shape, the shape has to put the right chemical parts near each other, and the final molecule has to bind, stay stable, or perform a biological role. A next-token model can produce a likely-looking string that fails those deeper checks.

State Space

The state is the protein as a connected object: the sequence letters, the identity of each amino acid, the folded shape, nearby contacts, binding pocket or partner molecule, target property, and the experiment or simulation used to judge it.

Object

The object is the set of protein candidates that remain biologically plausible. The letter sequence is only one description of the candidate. Shape, chemistry, binding context, and test setting are also part of what makes it valid. Changing the string is allowed only if those deeper checks still make sense.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is functional plausibility: the candidate should still fold, bind, stay stable, or satisfy the intended role. What may change is the exact sequence or local structure, as long as those deeper biological requirements survive.

Allowed Movement

The model may mutate residues, fill in missing pieces, decode a structure into a sequence, or condition on a target. It should not wander freely through strings when that freedom breaks folding or chemistry.

Why It Works

This works by reducing a hopeless search. All possible protein strings are too many, and most are useless. Biology gives layers of rules: nearby chemicals have to fit, the chain has to fold, the folded shape has to expose the right parts, and the final candidate has to work in a real test. Using those layers narrows the search to candidates that have a reason to work. The model is still making new candidates, but it is making them inside a region where biology has not already ruled them out. The formal term is constrained generative distribution: the crowd of generated candidates is shaped by biological rules before a candidate is treated as valid.

generate sequences only while fold, contacts, target property, and biological checks stay plausible

Evidence Check

Report fold consistency, preserved contacts, independent property predictions, binding or stability checks, novelty against known families, and lab or strong simulation evidence when claiming real biological function.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when likely-looking sequences are treated as valid proteins, when the constraint measures the wrong biological test, or when candidates fool a structure predictor without improving real function.

Connection

ReaSyn and MORetro* require molecule-building routes to be possible. Quotient-Space Diffusion removes irrelevant coordinate choices. IRNO preserves physical field structure. The shared move is to generate in the space where validity actually lives, not the space that is easiest to tokenize.

EXEMPLAR 27

Adam Degeneracy And Beyond Muon

Optimization As Choosing How The Model Is Allowed To Move

Problem

Training a model is like adjusting thousands of knobs at once. Two training methods can lower the error by the same amount while turning very different knobs. One may move gently in sensitive places; another may damage a useful internal feature while still making the average loss look good.

State Space

Imagine tuning a sound board after hearing what sounded wrong. The current mistake suggests which sliders to push first, but that suggestion does not know which sliders are delicate or which earlier pushes already happened. The state is the current setting of the model's knobs, the mistake-suggested move, the optimizer's memory of past moves, the fragile knob combinations, the next update, and what useful behavior changes afterward. The formal term is gradient: the direction the current errors point if we only ask how to lower them right now.

Object

The object is the rule for taking a step while training. Imagine tuning a sound board with thousands of sliders. Moving one slider by a millimeter may barely matter; moving another by the same amount may ruin the mix. The optimizer decides which proposed moves count as small, which count as dangerous, which should be amplified, and which should be muted. The formal term is optimizer-induced metric: the movement ruler created by the optimizer's own step rule.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful progress: training error should go down while the model keeps internal behavior needed for later tasks. What may change is update size, direction, scaling, or the coordinate system used to describe the move.

Allowed Movement

The optimizer may shrink, stretch, rotate, normalize, or project the update. It should move more where change is helpful and less where a tiny move can cause large damage. A sharp direction is like a hair-trigger slider: a small push changes the result a lot.

Why It Works

This works when the step rule matches the terrain. If the same learning-rate step is used everywhere, the model may crawl through forgiving directions and overshoot through fragile ones. Adam-style methods, Muon-style methods, and related analyses ask what ruler the optimizer is secretly using to measure a step. A better ruler makes the same proposed error-reducing push safer by resizing it according to how the model actually responds. The formal term is preconditioning: reshaping the update before applying it.

choose an update so training error goes down while sensitive directions are not moved too far

Evidence Check

Measure error decrease per amount moved, whether updates point in helpful directions, sensitivity to hair-trigger directions, what happens when optimizer memory is removed, how internal features drift, and whether gains remain after matching compute, batch size, and learning-rate schedules.

Failure Boundary

The argument fails when the movement rule mostly fits noise, when gains come only from better tuning, when average loss hides damage to later skills, or when the claimed step geometry is measured on a toy setting that does not match the real training run.

Connection

DiReCT chooses data by how it moves the model. LoRA theory asks whether a task can be learned by moving in a small set of directions. Trust-region RL limits policy movement. All say learning is not just "lower the loss"; it is controlled movement through the model.

EXEMPLAR 28

Transformer Circuits And Single-Head Attention

Architecture As Showing The Actual Computation

Problem

It is weak to say, "this model could solve the task." A calculator could in principle do many things, but we still need to know which buttons and operations produce the answer. For neural networks, the same issue appears: the architecture may be capable of a behavior, but we need to show the computation that actually creates it.

State Space

The state is the information moving through the network: how each token is stored, which earlier tokens are consulted, what gets copied, what gets combined, how each layer changes the working notes, and what training data made those routes useful. Attention scores are the model's learned weights for "which previous pieces matter for this piece."

Object

The object is a small working procedure inside the larger network. It may sort evidence, match repeated pieces, copy a name, compare two values, retrieve a memory, or refine a grouping. What must stay the same is the procedure from input to output. What may change is the exact weight pattern used to carry it out. The formal term is algorithmic circuit: a set of model parts that together carry out a recognizable procedure.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the input-to-output procedure: read the right pieces, combine them in the right order, update the working note, and produce the answer. The exact weights can differ if the same procedure still happens.

Allowed Movement

The analysis may ignore weight details that do not affect the procedure. It cannot ignore the links that make the procedure work, and it cannot claim a circuit from architecture alone. The claimed route has to be present in the trained model at the tested size, data setting, and numeric precision.

Why It Works

This works when the network can be read as a chain of concrete operations, not as a mystery box with a label on top. For clustering, one layer might assign points to centers, another might combine points into summaries, and another might refine the assignment. For single-head attention, the question is whether one head can find the repeated signal that matters while ignoring accidental noise. The everyday picture is hearing a steady beat in a noisy room: the beat keeps lining up, while random sounds do not. The formal term is spectral filtering: keeping the repeated pattern that lines up across the data while letting accidental variation fade.

the route claim is true only if the network's layers behave like the claimed procedure on the tested inputs

Evidence Check

Show hand-built settings or probes of learned mechanisms, remove the proposed circuit pieces, test inputs that keep the same rule but change surface details, track whether information moves through the claimed route, and separate "the network could do this" from "training actually made it do this."

Failure Boundary

The claim fails when the architecture could implement the algorithm but the trained model uses a shortcut, when a probe finds a pattern that the output does not depend on, or when the demonstrated mechanism only works at a scale, precision, or data setting unlike the real model.

Connection

Language Generation in the Limit separates what is possible from what can be learned from finite data. Alignment-Sensitive Minimax Rates asks when the target signal is visible. Finite certification asks what evidence proves the claim. Together they connect capability, learning, and proof.

EXEMPLAR 29

Finite Test Certification And Micro-Benchmark Reliability

Benchmarks As A Limited Amount Of Evidence

Problem

A benchmark score can look like a fact about a model: "Model A is better than Model B." But the score came from a limited set of questions, graders, prompts, and random model outputs. A small score gap may be a real difference, or it may be luck from the sampled questions.

State Space

The state is the public claim, the larger task world the claim is about, the finite questions actually sampled, the model answers, the grading rule, the score calculation, and the remaining uncertainty. The benchmark questions are only a sample of the broader task world, the way a handful of exam questions stands in for a whole subject. What must stay the same is the subject we are claiming to measure; what may change is the particular sampled questions. The formal term is task distribution: the larger world of cases the sampled benchmark is supposed to stand for.

Object

The object is a measured claim with a doubt range. A benchmark is like tasting a few spoonfuls from a large pot: the sample can tell you something, but only if the spoonfuls represent the pot and the taste difference is larger than ordinary variation. The score should say not only who won, but how much evidence that sampled test provides for the broader claim. What must stay the same is the meaning of the comparison on the stated task world; what may change is which finite items, graders, and prompts were sampled. The formal term is statistical certificate: a receipt saying what was checked, how much was checked, and how much uncertainty remains.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the meaning of the comparison. If the leaderboard says A is better, that should reflect a real expected difference on the stated task world, not a lucky draw of items or graders.

Allowed Movement

The evaluator may sample fewer items, choose items adaptively, combine subtasks, or split the score into parts. It must still report what population the claim covers and how uncertain the measurement is.

Why It Works

This works by replacing a naked score with a score plus a doubt range. More items, cleaner graders, and more stable prompts shrink that range because they remove ways the measured score could have been a lucky accident. A small benchmark does not become universal; it only tells us exactly how far the evidence reaches. If the measured gap is smaller than item noise, grader noise, prompt noise, or model randomness, the honest answer is "not enough evidence to rank them." The formal term is confidence interval: one way to state the remaining uncertainty around the measured score.

claim one model beats another only when the measured gap is larger than uncertainty from items, graders, prompts, and model randomness

Evidence Check

Report uncertainty ranges, rank stability after resampling, item-level variation, grader agreement, prompt sensitivity, model randomness, and whether the sampled tasks match the deployment claim.

Failure Boundary

The certificate fails when the benchmark is narrower than the public claim, when leaked items or biased grading distort the score, or when the uncertainty report ignores randomness from the model or evaluator.

Connection

ATLAS chooses questions that teach the most. FRABench separates score components. WebDevJudge and VERINA check artifacts directly. They all make evaluation more honest by saying what was actually measured and how much evidence it provides.

EXEMPLAR 30

Common Corpus And Hubble

Data As Training Evidence With Receipts

Problem

A model can improve after adding data, but the improvement may come from copied benchmark items, duplicated text, disallowed sources, or private material. If we do not know where the data came from, the score does not tell us what kind of evidence trained the behavior.

State Space

The state is the dataset with its source labels, licenses, collection dates, filters, duplicates, task labels, memorized spans, and downstream tests. A corpus is just the collected body of training text or examples.

Object

The object is not an anonymous pile of examples. It is a pantry with labels on every ingredient: where it came from, whether it is allowed, whether it is duplicated, and whether it has already appeared in the test. The same model score means different things if the improvement came from licensed general text, copied benchmark answers, private records, or repeated near-duplicates. What must stay the same is the audit trail from example to source; what may change is filtering, cleaning, balancing, or removal. The formal term is provenance: that source history.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is auditable training evidence. The pipeline may clean, filter, rebalance, or remove examples, but it should not erase the ability to answer: what did the model learn from, and was that source allowed?

Allowed Movement

The pipeline may remove duplicates, keep a subset, reweight sources, document sources, or exclude risky material. Those moves must preserve enough source history to support later claims about copied test items, privacy, rights, and model behavior.

Why It Works

This works because source identity turns a hidden training mixture into something inspectable. Once we know which source each example came from, we can ask whether that source helped, whether it copied test items, whether it caused memorization, and whether it was allowed. Without that receipt, a score gain cannot distinguish learning a general pattern from memorizing forbidden or duplicated evidence. The formal term is source distribution: the dataset recipe, including how much came from each source type, with what rights, filters, and duplication risk.

train on a documented source mix, with checks for license, copied tests, duplication, and memorization risk

Evidence Check

Report source weights, filtering rules, duplicate rates, memorization tests, source-removal tests, contamination checks, license accounting, and score changes after removing high-risk sources.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when source records are incomplete, when metadata is trusted without audit, or when score gains come from copied or disallowed material that the benchmark mistakes for general ability.

Connection

Sequential Data Values and Accuracy Auctions ask how much each data source contributes. Copyright-Bench turns legal rules into action boundaries. Privacy and unlearning papers ask whether a source can be hidden or removed. All require data to have receipts.

EXEMPLAR 31

TileLang, DHSA, And einx

Systems Work As Doing The Same Math Where The Machine Can Reach It

Problem

A recipe can have few steps and still be slow if every step makes you walk across the kitchen for one spoon. Neural-network code has the same problem. Counting arithmetic operations misses the cost of moving numbers through memory, arranging them for the chip, and launching the small machine programs that do the work.

State Space

The state is the real work the chip must do: the number tables the model needs, their shapes, where those tables sit in memory, which chunks are close enough to reuse, which small hardware routine runs, what rounding is used, and which chip executes it. A tensor is just a table of numbers. A kernel is a small low-level routine that repeats one piece of the calculation.

Object

The object is the same calculation placed onto a real machine without changing its meaning. Think of laying out ingredients for a recipe. Some ingredients must be prepared before others because later steps depend on them. Other steps can be grouped or moved closer together. The systems problem is to arrange the work so the chip can reach the needed numbers at the needed time while still producing the same answer. The formal term is computation graph: the dependency map of which calculations feed which later calculations.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the intended function: for the same inputs, the model should produce the same output up to acceptable rounding. What may change is the order, grouping, layout, or low-level expression of the calculation.

Allowed Movement

The implementation may split number tables into reusable blocks, combine small routines, reorder independent work, specialize for common shapes, or write the same equation in another form. It cannot break a required order, silently change rounding too much, or assume table shapes that users will not actually run.

Why It Works

This works because modern chips often wait on data movement more than arithmetic. A cook is faster when the ingredients for the next few steps are already on the counter; walking to the pantry for every spoonful dominates the work. Tiling means keeping a small block of numbers close to the processor long enough to reuse it. Fusion means doing several operations while those numbers are already nearby. What must stay the same is the calculation the model means to run; what may change is where blocks are stored, when they are loaded, and which operations are grouped. The formal term is memory hierarchy: the stack of close fast storage and farther slower storage.

keep the same number-table calculation while reducing expensive memory movement and unused hardware time

Evidence Check

Report hardware traces, how many bytes moved, how busy the chip stayed, shape sweeps, backend comparisons, numerical drift, full-model latency, and whether the speedup holds for the model sizes and input shapes users actually serve.

Failure Boundary

The claim fails when it is fast only for cherry-picked shapes, when setup or compilation overhead eats the win, when small numeric changes alter model behavior, or when one faster routine does not speed up the full pipeline users run.

Connection

LiftQuant and TetraJet change the numbers the machine stores. ThinKV changes what memory the model keeps. Finite-precision theory asks what computation survives machine limits. TileLang, DHSA, and einx make the machine part of the claim, not an afterthought.

EXEMPLAR 32

FOCUS And RePAIR

Pruning As Removing Parts Without Changing The Conversation Loop

Problem

Cutting parts out of a model can make it smaller while keeping short benchmark answers mostly right. But long generation is a loop: the model writes a token, then reads its own token as part of the next input. A tiny change can grow into repetition, topic collapse, or brittle answers after many steps.

State Space

The state is the full model, the smaller model, the weights or channels removed, the next-token choices over time, the generated context that feeds back into the model, and behavior measures such as repetition and drift.

Object

The object is a self-feeding writing loop. The model chooses a next word, that word becomes part of the text it reads next, and the new context changes later word choices. Pruning is risky because removing one part of the model can slightly change early word choices, and those changes can become the new road the model keeps following. The formal term is dynamical system: a process where each step changes what the next step sees.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is behavior across a whole generated path, not only the first next-word score. What may change is the number of model parts, as long as repeated generation still stays stable.

Allowed Movement

The method may remove weights, repair internal numbers, or adjust the smaller model. It must keep the next-step rule from creating traps such as repeated phrases, topic collapse, or overconfident dead ends. That next-step rule turns the current context into chances for the next token, and then the chosen token becomes part of the next context. The formal term is transition kernel: the rule that turns the current state into the next-step odds.

Why It Works

Repair works when it restores the stabilizing job that the removed parts used to do. The issue is not one small error at one input. The issue is whether that error keeps feeding itself until the model falls into a bad loop. A slightly wrong steering wheel alignment may be barely visible for one meter and obvious after a mile; pruning has the same long-path risk. What must stay the same is the stable writing loop over many steps. What may change is which weights or channels remain. The right test follows the generated path long enough to see whether small differences grow.

audit whether small next-token changes in the pruned model accumulate into repeated or collapsed states

Evidence Check

Measure repetition rates, long-generation drift, diversity over time, topic collapse, recovery after repair, layer-removal tests, and cases where the smaller model matches short scores but behaves differently in long outputs.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when repair fixes obvious repetition but misses other drift, when tests are too short to reveal the loop, or when pruning removes rare abilities that matter for safety or reliability.

Connection

Quantized diffusion studies small errors that accumulate along a sampling path. LiftQuant studies machine-number errors. RAGEN-2 studies reasoning traces collapsing during training. FOCUS and RePAIR make the same point for compressed language models: preserve the running process, not just the static score.

EXEMPLAR 33

VALUEFLOW And Accuracy Auctions

Governance As Rules That Change How People Act

Problem

If a system pays people for data, ranks people by a score, or turns preferences into policy, people will react to the rule. They may report honestly, exaggerate, hide information, coordinate with others, or change behavior to get a better outcome. An average reward can hide who benefits and who pays.

State Space

The state is the people or groups involved, what each knows privately, what they report, what data they contribute, how the system allocates benefits or payments, what fairness or welfare goal is claimed, and what constraints must be respected.

Object

The object is the rule connecting reports to outcomes. A classroom grading rule changes how students study; a data-payment rule changes what sellers submit; a leaderboard changes what teams optimize. The rule is part of the system because people respond to it. What must stay the same is the intended social property after people react, such as honesty, fairness, welfare, or useful allocation; what may change is bids, reports, rankings, payments, or contributions. The formal term is mechanism: a rule that turns people's reports or contributions into payments, rankings, allocations, or policy choices.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is legitimacy after people respond to incentives. A result is not meaningful if it works only when everyone behaves in a way the rule itself discourages.

Allowed Movement

The designer may average, weight, price, restrict, or redistribute outcomes. The page has to say which property is being protected: honest reporting, fair treatment, efficient allocation, total welfare, or protection for a subgroup.

Why It Works

This works when the rule makes useful behavior the sensible behavior. If honesty gives people worse outcomes than manipulation, the system will not get honest information. If a metric rewards hiding hard cases, the reported score stops meaning performance. The point is to design the rule while accounting for how participants will try to improve their own outcomes under that rule. The formal term is mechanism design: designing the rule while modeling how people or agents will react to it.

choose a rule where the useful or truthful action is the sensible response under the stated assumptions

Evidence Check

Report simulations where participants game the rule, subgroup benefit and harm curves, collusion checks, sensitivity to strong preferences, payment robustness, and examples where improving one group hurts another.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when real incentives are outside the model, when one welfare number hides who is harmed, or when the assumed private information does not match what people actually know or control.

Connection

ParetoPO exposes cost tradeoffs. SafeDPO exposes safety-usefulness tradeoffs. Data markets price evidence. Fair Optimal Transport constrains allocation. Governance papers add the human layer: the measurement rule changes the system being measured.

EXEMPLAR 34

LIMSSR And Robust Missing-Covariate Optimization

Decision Making When Important Facts Are Missing

Problem

A doctor, lender, or robot often has to decide with incomplete information. The missing fact may be exactly the fact that matters. If a model silently fills it in and acts confident, the decision can look precise while resting on a guess.

State Space

The state is the full set of facts that would matter if we had them, the subset we actually see, the pattern of what is missing, any guessed value, the decision rule, the score, and the uncertainty about unseen possibilities. A background fact may change what decision is fair or accurate, even if that fact is not the main thing being predicted. The formal term is covariate: a background fact used to make the decision or comparison fairer.

Object

The object is a decision made from partial evidence. A doctor who has symptoms but no lab result should not act as if the lab result were known; the missing result defines several possible patient states. The decision should use what is visible while keeping track of what could still be true in the hidden part. What must stay the same is honest uncertainty about the missing facts; what may change is the action, score, abstention, or range of plausible hidden completions. The formal term is observed-subset decision problem: a decision tied to exactly the facts the system can see.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is honest uncertainty about the missing part. The model may infer likely hidden facts, but it should not turn a guess into unsupported certainty.

Allowed Movement

The method may encode what is visible, average over possible missing values, protect against bad hidden cases, or produce a conditional score. The final decision must remain valid for the missingness pattern it claims to handle. Imputation means filling in a missing value; robust means the decision is tested against more than one possible fill-in.

Why It Works

This works by separating evidence from completion. The observed facts say what is known. The missing facts define a range of possible worlds. A careful decision asks what action is still reasonable across that range, rather than pretending the most convenient completion is true.

choose an action that performs acceptably over the plausible hidden states consistent with the observed facts

Evidence Check

Report tests with structured missing facts, calibration when a whole input type is absent, fill-in ablations, worst-case loss, shifts in missingness patterns, and cases where the model abstains or widens uncertainty instead of inventing certainty.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when missingness is treated as harmless random absence but is actually tied to the outcome, when the uncertainty range leaves out realistic hidden states, or when the model learns shortcuts from dataset-specific missing markers.

Connection

VISUALSWAP asks which input channel carries evidence. Local Covariate Selection asks which background facts make a comparison fair. Mind-Omni asks whether a biological signal channel is real. Missingness work asks the same question when the channel is absent.

EXEMPLAR 35

Copyright-Bench And Legal Agents

Law As Knowing Which Helpful Moves Are Allowed

Problem

An agent can be useful in a way that is not allowed. It may quote too much, transform protected material in the wrong setting, give legal-sounding advice without enough context, or refuse everything near the boundary. The hard part is not "be helpful" or "be safe" in general; it is choosing the right move for the specific content and rule.

State Space

The state is the user's request, the source material, the relevant rule or policy, the action the agent wants to take, the final output, whether it refuses or complies, and the risk label for that situation.

Object

The object is the set of responses the rule still permits. A helpful move is not useful if it crosses a legal boundary, and a refusal is not good if an allowed answer was available. Each possible answer is allowed, forbidden, or close enough to the line that it needs extra care. What must stay the same is usefulness inside the rule; what may change is summary, transformation, citation, clarification, refusal, or safe alternative. The formal term is constrained action set: the remaining menu after forbidden actions are removed.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is usefulness within the rule. The system should not become helpful by crossing the legal boundary, and it should not become "safe" by refusing every hard but allowed case.

Allowed Movement

The agent may summarize, transform, cite, ask for clarification, refuse, or offer an allowed alternative. The right choice depends on the source, the user's request, the jurisdiction or policy, and the kind of transformation.

Why It Works

This works when evaluation turns vague legal pressure into concrete boundary examples. The model has to learn two things: first, whether a response is allowed for this source, request, and rule; second, what useful action remains if the first choice is not allowed. The checker is really asking whether a candidate answer sits inside or outside the allowed region. The formal term is feasible-region classifier: the checker that decides whether a candidate answer sits inside the allowed region.

choose the most useful response among the responses allowed for this source, request, and rule

Evidence Check

Report false refusals, false allowances, near-boundary examples, sensitivity to source length, transformation type, citation behavior, and prompts that ask for the same content in different wording.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when labels are too coarse, when legal context is missing, when generic refusal is rewarded too much, or when the model handles obvious examples but fails nearby variants.

Connection

CSPO and SafeDPO also restrict model movement. Common Corpus makes source rights part of data state. WebDevJudge checks real artifacts. Legal-agent work adds a social rule layer: some outputs are unavailable even if the model can produce them.

EXEMPLAR 36

VideoKR And Visual Attribution Streaming

Long Video As Remembering The Frame That Matters

Problem

A long video may answer a question in one brief moment: someone picks up a key, a car changes lanes, a patient shows a symptom, or an object moves behind a door. A model can sound right by using captions or nearby frames while missing the actual moment that supports the answer.

State Space

The state is the stream of frames, events, tracked objects, the user's question, the memory kept by the model, the evidence retrieved later, the path from evidence to answer, and the answer itself.

Object

The object is a time-ordered memory of visual evidence. It should keep the frame or event that can change the answer. The memory is not a scrapbook of every frame; it is closer to a security guard's incident log: what happened, when it happened, which object was involved, and why that moment may matter later. The formal term is temporal evidence state: the saved visual record whose order and event links still support later answers.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is answer-relevant visual evidence. The model may discard most frames, but not the rare event that decides the question.

Allowed Movement

The method may split the video into events, retrieve old frames, mark which frames mattered, summarize, or update memory as frames arrive. It must keep the order links that matter: before versus after, cause versus result, and object identity across frames.

Why It Works

This works when the kept memory is the evidence a later answer actually uses. If the question is "who picked up the key," the decisive moment may last less than a second; a summary that keeps only common scenes can miss it. The method earns trust when removing or swapping the highlighted moment changes the answer in the expected way. The formal term is attribution: assigning credit to the input parts that caused the output.

keep video memory that changes the answer when the supporting event is removed or swapped

Evidence Check

Use delayed-evidence questions, frame swaps, event deletion, checks that attribution is faithful, memory-budget curves, question-conditioned retrieval audits, and cases where text priors conflict with what the video shows.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when it highlights plausible-looking frames rather than answer-causing frames, keeps common events while dropping rare decisive events, or answers benchmark questions without needing the video.

Connection

ThinKV compresses text context. VISUALSWAP tests which input channel carried evidence. DLMR tracks memory sources. VideoKR and streaming attribution combine all three: long memory, visual grounding, and evidence route.

EXEMPLAR 37

SAW-Bench And RoboMME

Embodied Benchmarks As Measuring Whether The World Changed

Problem

A benchmark can ask a robot question without testing robotic ability. If the score rewards a written plan but never checks perception, contact, motion, or the final world state, it can make a model look embodied while measuring language only.

State Space

The state is the environment, what the agent perceives, the instruction, the action sequence, the intermediate world states, the success condition, and the trace of what failed.

Object

The object is a task attempt over time, not a static answer. A written plan to put a cup on a shelf is not the same as seeing the cup, reaching it, grasping it, avoiding obstacles, and leaving it on the shelf. The score should watch what the agent saw, what it tried, how the world changed, and where the attempt ended. What must stay the same is physical task success through real state changes; what may change is whether the evidence is logged by simulator, robot sensors, images, or language. The formal term is trajectory: the path of situation, action, next situation, and so on until success or failure.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is physical task success: did perception support the right action, did the action change the world, and did the final state satisfy the goal?

Allowed Movement

The evaluator may split the task into parts, use simulation, or include images and language. The score must still depend on actual state changes, not only whether the plan sounds plausible.

Why It Works

This works because physical failures become visible only when the attempt is followed through time. The agent may choose an object it cannot reach, lose contact, treat a closed drawer like an open one, or fail to recover after a bump. The benchmark has to ask whether the world offered the required action in that moment: a handle can be grasped, a drawer can be opened, a path can be crossed, or a cup can be lifted without slipping. The formal term is affordance: the action the current world state makes possible for the agent.

score the full action path, including whether each action caused the needed world change

Evidence Check

Report state-change logs, contact failures, impossible-action failures, simulator-to-real sensitivity, input ablations, recovery after perturbations, and disagreement between written-plan score and executed success.

Failure Boundary

The benchmark fails when environments are too scripted, when answers can be inferred from text alone, when perception artifacts leak labels, or when physical limits are absent from the score.

Connection

HDFlow and PACT constrain action steps. BehaviorVLA learns reusable behavior. WebDevJudge checks web artifacts in their native environment. Embodied benchmarks apply the same principle to physical action: check the thing in the medium where it can fail.

EXEMPLAR 38

Language Generation In The Limit

Formal Possibility Versus Evidence That Can Find It

Problem

A lock may have a correct key, but that does not mean you can find it by trying a few keys. A model may be capable of representing a language rule, but realistic training data may not contain enough evidence to identify that rule.

State Space

The state is the class of possible languages, the process generating examples, the learner, the candidate rule the learner believes, the error standard, and the amount of data available. A formal language is a precisely defined set of allowed strings.

Object

The object is the amount of evidence needed before learning is possible. If many different rules fit the examples seen so far, the learner has not been given enough evidence to know which rule is right. The question is not just whether a rule exists; it is how many examples are needed before the wrong rules can be ruled out. The formal term is sample complexity: how much evidence is needed before a learner can reliably find the right rule.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the distinction between "there exists a rule" and "the learner can find the rule from the available data." Possible in principle is not the same as learnable from evidence.

Allowed Movement

The theorem may choose the language family, the learner's access to data, the success condition, and the kind of convergence. It must state which evidence setting it is talking about and what generation guarantee is being claimed.

Why It Works

The argument works by showing that many different rules can agree on the examples seen so far while disagreeing later. A few keys fitting the first pins of a lock does not prove which key opens the door. If the data cannot separate the remaining rules, no learner can know which one is right within that budget. What must stay the same is the distinction between possible and evidenced; what may change is model architecture, language family, or sample access. The formal terms are expressivity, meaning whether the model can represent a rule, and learnability, meaning whether evidence can select the right one.

finite samples cannot identify the target if many possible languages match the samples but differ elsewhere

Evidence Check

State the language family, what the learner observes, the success criterion, sampling assumptions, lower-bound example, and whether the impossibility is about worst-case formal classes or practical data with extra structure.

Failure Boundary

The warning is overused when a worst-case barrier is applied to narrower real settings where structure makes learning easier. It is underused when architecture capability is advertised as if it already solved the evidence problem.

Connection

Transformer Circuits shows possible computations. Finite Test Certification asks what finite evidence proves. ATLAS treats evaluation as measurement. Language Generation in the Limit supplies the missing caution: a representable rule is not necessarily a learnable rule.

EXEMPLAR 39

The Tell-Tale Norm

Internal Magnitude As A Sign Of When To Think More

Problem

Some questions are easy and some need extra work. Spending extra computation on every question wastes time, but never spending it misses hard cases. The question is whether the model has an internal sign that says, "this is one of the cases where more thinking may help."

State Space

The state is the prompt, the model's internal numbers at each layer, the size of those internal numbers, the rule for trying more computation, the candidate answers, and final answer quality. Activations are the internal numbers a layer produces; a norm is a single size measure for a group of numbers.

Object

The object is a measured internal sign used for a decision. It is not the reasoning itself. It is more like a dashboard light that may tell us when the model is uncertain, effortful, or in a state where extra computation is valuable. The number comes from hidden model state, so it must earn trust by predicting real improvement, not by sounding psychologically meaningful. The formal term is latent-state statistic: a number measured from hidden model state and used as evidence for what to do next.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful information about effort or uncertainty. If the internal size changes, it should track real reasoning demand or confidence, not answer length, topic, style, or refusal wording.

Allowed Movement

The system may run another reasoning pass, steer the model, sample another answer, or choose among answers based on that internal sign. Those interventions must improve real tasks at the same compute budget, not merely make the model look more thoughtful.

Why It Works

This works only if the internal sign predicts the value of extra computation. A dashboard light is useful only if it points to a real engine condition, not if it turns on whenever the road is long. If high norm means "extra work is likely to reduce error," then compute can be spent selectively. The signal must still help after ordinary clues like prompt length, topic, and answer style are already considered. What must stay the same is real information about uncertainty or effort; what may change is whether the system thinks again, samples again, or stops. The formal term is conditional information: information the internal sign adds after the visible prompt clues are already known.

spend extra compute when the internal signal predicts enough error reduction to justify the cost

Evidence Check

Measure which layers carry the signal, whether norm predicts errors, improvement per extra compute, off-target effects, stability under prompt changes, and whether norm-guided extra work beats giving every question the same extra work.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the norm reflects verbosity, domain, fake uncertainty, or prompt artifacts instead of useful reasoning state, or when the intervention pushes the model into internal states it was not trained to handle.

Connection

Steer Like the LLM changes internal directions. RAGEN-2 checks whether traces depend on the input. Mind-Omni asks whether biological internal signals carry evidence. All turn hidden state into something measurable, then require proof that the measurement actually matters.

EXEMPLAR 40

h1 And OpenThoughts

Long Reasoning As Teaching Short Links Before Long Chains

Problem

Long reasoning is hard because the final answer may depend on many earlier steps. If the model only hears "right" or "wrong" at the end, it may not know which earlier step helped or failed. The learning signal becomes weak by the time it reaches the beginning of the chain.

State Space

The state is the set of smaller subtasks, the intermediate conclusions, training examples, reasoning traces, how many steps are chained together, the feedback signal, and the updates made to the model. A dependency means one step must be correct because a later step uses it.

Object

The object is the dependency chain inside the work. Think of following a recipe where the dough must rise before baking: if the early step fails, the final cake cannot be fixed by decorating it well. Each intermediate piece becomes an input to something later, so a small early mistake can poison the rest of the chain. The useful map shows the pieces and which later pieces depend on them. The formal term is task graph: the map of work pieces and the dependency links between them.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is dependency correctness. A short-step skill must remain useful when it becomes part of a longer problem; otherwise the long trace is just more text, not more reasoning.

Allowed Movement

The curriculum may start with short chains, increase the length, combine examples, or use reasoning traces. It must preserve the real dependencies that make the task longer, not just add verbose filler.

Why It Works

The reason is the same as teaching arithmetic before algebra word problems. The model first learns local moves where feedback is close to the action. Then the curriculum composes those moves into longer chains. What must stay the same is that a step learned alone still does the same job when a later step relies on it. What may change is the chain length, trace style, or mix of examples. The hard part is blame and credit: when the final answer is wrong, the training signal needs a way to point back to the step that caused the failure. The formal term is credit assignment: deciding which earlier step should receive responsibility for the final success or failure.

learn short dependable steps first, then increase chain length while feedback still identifies which step mattered

Evidence Check

Run tests by chain length, scramble dependencies, perturb traces, test transfer from short to long compositions, compare sparse versus step-level feedback, and check that long traces actually use earlier intermediate results instead of merely sounding longer.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when long tasks share shallow templates, when the model memorizes trace style, when feedback cannot identify the broken step, or when a short-step skill breaks once later steps interact with it.

Connection

Midtraining Bridges chooses when data should enter training. Skill-Pro turns successful traces into reusable procedures. DiReCT chooses examples by how they move the model. h1 and OpenThoughts make the reasoning-side lesson explicit: make the learning path navigable.

EXEMPLAR 41

Omni-Reward And JudgeBench

Reward Models As Scores That Must Still Mean Quality

Problem

Quality is messy. A judge model turns it into a number so another model can train against it. The danger is that the model learns how to get a high number without improving the thing people actually wanted, such as truth, usefulness, safety, or visual correctness.

State Space

The state is the answer or artifact, the real quality we care about, what the judge can observe, the reward score, the training process that pushes outputs toward high scores, the evidence in each input type, and behavior after the model has learned to chase the score.

Object

The object is a noisy measurement of quality. The real target may be truth, usefulness, safety, or visual correctness, but the training loop sees an easier stand-in score. That score is like a thermometer: it is useful only while it still rises when the real thing rises. If the score goes up while real quality goes down, training is now improving the measuring device's blind spot instead of the target. The formal terms are proxy, meaning the stand-in measurement, and reward channel, meaning the path from real quality, through judge observations, into the number used for training.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the link between higher reward and real improvement. What may change is the output style, ranking, or training path, as long as the reward still tracks the target under pressure.

Allowed Movement

The system may train, rank, filter, or guide outputs using reward scores. But the score must be audited after the model has learned to chase it, because that is when the model has the strongest incentive to exploit judge blind spots.

Why It Works

Reward modeling works when the score keeps the same order as real quality in the region the trained model visits. If better answers usually get higher scores, the reward is useful. If high-scoring answers become glossy but false, the reward has broken. The danger appears after training teaches the model to produce answer styles the judge never had to grade before. The formal term is distribution shift: the graded cases after training differ from the cases where the measurement was known to work.

use reward only while higher reward still means higher target quality on trained outputs

Evidence Check

Measure reward-target disagreement, judge calibration, reward hacking, audits after the model has learned to chase the score, dependence on visual or other non-text evidence, pairwise consistency, and human review of high-reward boundary cases.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the reward sees surface fluency instead of target quality, ignores important input channels, or drives the model into cases where the judge is confidently wrong.

Connection

RLVepsR studies verifier labels as noisy training pressure. WebDevJudge checks real artifacts. Safety-Guided Flow uses reward while sampling. Omni-Reward and JudgeBench name the central risk: the training target is only as trustworthy as the measurement behind it.

EXEMPLAR 42

OPUS And HOBIT

Data Selection As Choosing Examples By What They Do To The Model

Problem

An example is not valuable by itself. It is valuable because training on it changes the model. The same example can teach something new early, become redundant later, or push the model in a harmful direction after the model has already learned related material.

State Space

The state is the current model, candidate examples, the change each example would cause, the optimizer's memory, the held-out behavior goal, fragile model directions, and downstream performance. Each example creates a training push: it asks the model to move so that this example is handled better. The formal term is gradient: that push written as a direction for changing the model.

Object

The object is the extra value of training on one example now. A lesson is useful only relative to the student in front of it: too early, it has no foundation; too late, it repeats what is already known; aimed badly, it teaches the wrong habit. An example matters if its update teaches something the current model still needs and does not cause too much damage elsewhere. The formal term is marginal update value: the extra improvement caused by adding this example at this moment.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful model movement per unit of data or compute. Selected examples should push the model toward the target without causing too much damage elsewhere.

Allowed Movement

The method may rank, filter, weight, or order examples. The decision should be based on the actual update the training rule will make, not just whether the example looks similar, hard, rare, or related in meaning.

Why It Works

This works because training depends on the path already taken. Optimizer-aware data selection estimates how an example will move the current model, then keeps examples whose movement helps the held-out behavior goal. What must stay the same is useful movement toward the target. What may change is which examples are kept, weighted, or delayed. Some model directions are fragile: a tiny push there can change behavior a lot, while the same-sized push elsewhere barely matters. Useful examples must therefore be judged by both benefit and damage risk. The formal terms are curvature or sensitivity: how strongly behavior changes when the model is nudged in a particular direction.

select examples whose predicted update improves the held-out behavior goal while limiting off-target movement

Evidence Check

Report whether predicted updates match actual validation gains, phase sensitivity, redundancy tests, comparison to static filters, off-target loss, fragile-direction movement, and whether selected examples remain useful under optimizer or learning-rate changes.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when update estimates are too noisy, when held-out behavior goals are too narrow, when selected examples overfit a temporary training phase, or when the optimizer used for scoring differs from the optimizer used for training.

Connection

DiReCT chooses data by how it moves the model. Midtraining Bridges chooses when data should enter. Common Corpus tracks where data came from. OPUS and HOBIT complete the data story by asking what each example does to the model right now.

EXEMPLAR 43

Gaussian Certified Unlearning

Deletion As Making Two Histories Look The Same

Problem

When someone asks for their data to be removed, we cannot open a trained model and see a labeled drawer for that example. The model may look changed for unrelated reasons, or it may look unchanged while still leaking something about the removed data.

State Space

The state is the original training data, the deletion request, the original training method, the unlearning method, a reference model retrained without the deleted data, the released model, what an auditor or attacker can see, and the test used to compare the two worlds.

Object

The object is a comparison between two histories: one where the data was never used, and one where it was used and then removed. Each history can lead to many possible model outputs because training has randomness and the model can answer in different ways. The question is whether those two clouds of possible outputs overlap so much that an observer cannot tell which history happened. The formal term is distribution: the cloud of possible outputs and how much chance sits on each one.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the meaning of deletion. After unlearning, an observer should not be able to reliably tell which history happened, within the access rules the guarantee claims.

Allowed Movement

The unlearning method may update, perturb, or randomize the model. It may add noise. It may lose some usefulness. But the released model must behave close to a model that was properly trained without the deleted data.

Why It Works

This works when noise and updates limit what an observer can learn about the deleted example. The guarantee does not prove the model has human-like forgetting. It proves the two histories are hard to tell apart: the observer should not be able to tell whether the data was never used or was used and then removed. The proof is about tests, not feelings of forgetting: no allowed test should reliably separate the two output clouds. The formal term is statistical indistinguishability: the two histories look so similar to allowed tests that the test cannot reliably tell them apart.

unlearning is certified when the released model is hard to distinguish from retraining without the deleted data

Evidence Check

Report what the attacker can observe, how close the two histories are, utility loss, deletion batch size, dimension effects, repeated deletion behavior, and membership or reconstruction attacks.

Failure Boundary

The deletion guarantee fails when attackers see more than the guarantee assumed, when the added noise is too small for large models with many internal numbers, or when preserving usefulness forces the method to keep data-specific traces.

Connection

Common Corpus tracks where data came from. Watermarking wants selected traces to remain detectable. Privacy wants private traces to be hard to detect. Unlearning is the deletion version: prove a source no longer leaves an auditable difference.

EXEMPLAR 44

Catch-22 And Watermarking

Watermarking As Hiding A Mark That Still Survives Editing

Problem

A watermark has to do three things at once: let the owner detect it, survive edits like paraphrasing or cropping, and stay hard for an attacker to find and remove. Those goals conflict. A stronger mark is easier to detect, but also easier to notice.

State Space

The state is the generated text, image, or audio; the secret key; the rule that places the mark; the detector; the edits or attacks applied later; the unmarked case; the marked case; and the cutoff for saying "mark found."

Object

The object is a test between two explanations: this artifact came from the marked generator, or it did not. The detector looks at evidence in the artifact and chooses the explanation that better fits. The key question is how often the detector falsely accuses clean artifacts and how often it misses marked ones after normal edits. The formal term is hypothesis test: a rule for choosing between rival explanations while tracking the two kinds of mistake.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is reliable origin evidence. The mark must leave enough signal to be found after ordinary edits, while not damaging quality or making removal easy.

Allowed Movement

The system may slightly change word choices, image statistics, internal features, or signatures. The artifact should still look and work normally, and the test must match the attacks it claims to survive.

Why It Works

This works when the detector measures a pattern that appears much more often in marked artifacts than unmarked ones, even after edits. It breaks when editing blurs that pattern or when the pattern is visible enough for attackers to erase. The detector finally reduces the pattern to a number and compares that number with a cutoff. The formal term is detection statistic: the number made from the artifact pattern and used to decide whether the mark is present.

detect a mark when the measured pattern is common after marked generation and rare without it

Evidence Check

Report false alarms, missed marks, survival under attacks, quality loss, risk that the secret key is exposed, adaptive removal tests, shifts to new data, and detection after paraphrase, crop, compression, or regeneration.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when robustness makes the mark visible to attackers, when stealth makes detection too weak, or when evaluation uses easier edits than realistic laundering.

Connection

Gaussian unlearning tries to make two histories hard to distinguish. Watermarking tries to make selected origins distinguishable. SecFid studies security versus normal-use quality. Common Corpus asks whether origin can be audited before generation. All are evidence tests with different desired mistakes.

EXEMPLAR 45

Fair Optimal Transport

Fairness As Choosing Allowed Matches Before Minimizing Cost

Problem

Suppose a system assigns people to schools, jobs, loans, or services. The cheapest matching can still place most burden on one group. If fairness is checked only after the cheapest match is chosen, the unfair choices were already allowed during the search.

State Space

The state is the people or items being assigned, the destinations or outcomes, the cost of each possible match, group labels or protected structure, the full matching table, fairness rules, and final allocation.

Object

The object is the assignment spreadsheet itself. Picture assigning students to schools: each row is a student group, each column is a school, and each filled cell says how much assignment goes there. In allocation, the same table decides which people get which opportunities or burdens. The table is the real decision object because it decides who is paired with what, how much cost they carry, and whether the fairness rule is obeyed before cost is minimized. The formal terms are transport plan, meaning the full movement table, and coupling, meaning the pairing table that says which source is linked to which target and how much.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful allocation with fairness meaning. The plan should still match well, but it must not concentrate cost, harm, or benefit in a forbidden pattern.

Allowed Movement

The method may change pairings, move more or less amount through different matches, or smooth the plan. What must stay the same is that the required people, items, or probability mass are fully assigned and the stated fairness rule is satisfied. What may change is which allowed table gives the lowest cost.

Why It Works

This works because the fairness rule is placed on the object that creates outcomes: the matching table itself. Instead of choosing an unfair plan and trying to patch it later, the method removes unfair plans from the candidate set before optimizing cost. That matters because a cheap assignment can hide harm inside specific cells of the table. The method asks first, "which tables are allowed at all?" and only then asks, "which allowed table is cheapest?"

choose the lowest-cost matching among matchings that satisfy the fairness rule

Evidence Check

Report cost-versus-fairness curves, subgroup outcomes, sensitivity to the fairness rule, feasibility under data shifts, comparison to after-the-fact repair, and which harm the rule actually prevents or merely moves elsewhere.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when group labels are incomplete, when the fairness rule misses the real harm, or when small data shifts make every fair plan impossible or too costly.

Connection

CSPO restricts model movement. VALUEFLOW shows welfare tradeoffs. ParetoPO shows cost-accuracy tradeoffs. Fair Optimal Transport applies the same first principle to allocation: decide which moves are allowed before choosing the cheapest one.

EXEMPLAR 46

Mamba Markov In-Context Learning

In-Context Learning As Keeping A Running Summary Of The Task

Problem

A model that reads examples one after another cannot keep an unlimited prompt word for word. To learn from the examples in the prompt, it needs to keep a compact running summary of what task seems to be happening.

State Space

The state is the sequence of examples, the model's running internal memory, the rule for updating that memory, the hidden task the examples reveal, noise in the examples, and the next prediction.

Object

The object is the running summary that is enough for the next prediction. Imagine watching examples of a game and keeping a scorecard of the rule you think is being used. You do not need to copy every move, but you do need the clue that changes the next move. Enough means the next prediction from the summary should match the prediction the model would make if it reread the useful parts of the full context. Step-by-step means the summary is revised after each new example. The formal term is sufficient recurrent statistic: a running memory that keeps the task information needed for future predictions.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the task clue carried by the context. The model may forget exact examples, but it should keep the pattern that decides the next answer. After each example, the model's current guess about the hidden task should become better informed. The formal term is posterior: the revised belief after seeing the examples so far, like updating a scorecard after each new clue.

Allowed Movement

The model may update its compact memory after each example. What may change is the smaller running note. What must stay the same is the evidence needed for future predictions. The update should not confuse irrelevant order, surface wording, or repeated filler with the real task pattern.

Why It Works

This works when the hidden memory behaves like a good scorecard for the task. Each new example changes the scorecard only where it teaches something about the rule. The summary is good if it keeps everything the future prediction needs; then the raw past can be compressed without losing the task. The formal term is Markov structure: the current running state carries the needed past, so the next prediction does not need the full old sequence again.

a running state is good when predictions from that state match predictions from the full context

Evidence Check

Test delayed evidence, order sensitivity, memory-size limits, tasks where the current state should or should not be enough, longer prompts, update-rule ablations, and whether hidden memory recovers the task pattern rather than only recent wording.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the task cannot be compressed into the assumed summary, when early irrelevant examples overwrite later evidence, or when long contexts require direct retrieval instead of a running memory.

Connection

ThinKV keeps sufficient context tokens. VideoKR stores visual evidence over time. Skill-Pro compresses agent traces into procedures. Mamba Markov ICL gives the theory-side version of the same question: what state is enough?

EXEMPLAR 47

ThunderAgent, Speculative Actions, And ADP

Agent Latency As Doing Safe Future Work Early

Problem

Agents are often slow because the time is not only in writing text. They call tools, wait for websites, retrieve files, check results, and update external state. Making token generation faster does not fix a workflow where the slow parts are outside the model.

State Space

Think of a repair shop preparing likely parts while diagnosis is still running. Some preparation is safe because unused parts can go back on the shelf; other actions must wait because they change the customer's car. The workflow record includes model calls, likely next branches, tool state, side effects, checker gates, cached context, and reusable traces.

Object

The object is a work plan with real dependencies. Some steps can happen early because they do not depend on the answer to another step. Some steps must wait because doing them early would use stale facts or cause side effects. The useful map says which work can be prepared, which work can run side by side, and which work can be accepted only after a checker confirms the current state. The formal term is execution graph: the dependency map of work steps, outside actions, checker gates, and side-effect boundaries.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the correct task state. What may change is scheduling: likely future work may be prepared, branches may run in parallel, and old traces may be reused, but no irreversible tool action should happen before the evidence needed to approve it exists.

Allowed Movement

The system may predict future actions, prefetch data, run branches in parallel, cache results, or normalize traces. It should do this only when a checker can accept or reject the early work before it affects the official state, and only when the early work keeps enough context to debug or undo the choice.

Why It Works

This works when accepted early work saves more time than rejected early work wastes. Preparing a likely file read is useful if the result can be discarded cheaply. Sending a payment, editing a database, or submitting a form is different because the outside world changes. The method works by separating reversible preparation from committed action, then checking whether the prepared branch matches the later evidence. The formal term is speculative execution: preparing a likely branch before knowing for sure it will be needed, then keeping it only if later evidence agrees.

prepare early work only when likely saved time exceeds wrong-branch waste plus checker cost

Evidence Check

Report accepted speculation rate, wrong-branch cost, side-effect safety, stale-state failures, end-to-end latency, task success under concurrency, rollback behavior, and whether trace reuse preserves debugging information.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when speculative actions have irreversible side effects, branch prediction is weak, checker costs are too high, cached state grows stale, or normalized traces remove state needed for correctness later.

Connection

ParetoPO measures tool cost. Skill-Pro reuses verified procedures. WebDevJudge checks native execution. ThunderAgent and speculative-action work make agent speed a workflow scheduling problem, not just a UI latency problem.

EXEMPLAR 48

RAIN-Merging, FlexRank, And LoRA Geometry

Adaptation As Adding A Skill Without Moving Through Fragile Parts

Problem

A model can learn a new task and accidentally damage an old ability, a safety habit, or a reasoning style. The damage is often not random. It happens because the update moved through parts of the model that were carrying something important.

State Space

The state is the base model, proposed changes from different tasks, adapter pieces, the budget for how many direction patterns can change, fragile feature directions, target behavior, and tests for retained behavior.

Object

The object is the allowed set of ways the model may change. Imagine renovating one room in a house without cutting through load-bearing walls. The new room can change, but the support structure must remain. Instead of letting every internal knob move freely, the method limits the update to a smaller family of directions where the new skill is expected to fit while fragile old behavior is left mostly untouched. The formal terms are subspace, meaning the smaller family of allowed directions, and low rank, meaning the change is built from a limited number of direction patterns.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful old behavior while new behavior is added. The model may change on target tasks, but it should avoid directions that carry general reasoning, safety, or other retained capabilities.

Allowed Movement

The method may merge task updates, remove risky parts of an update, choose how many direction patterns are allowed, or factor adapters into smaller pieces. What must stay the same is retained old behavior. What may change is the new-task behavior and the small set of internal directions used to add it.

Why It Works

The method works when useful new-task movement can be separated from movement that damages retained behavior. The limited update is a budget: it says the model may change through only a few patterns instead of every possible knob. That can protect old behavior if the protected behavior really lives outside those patterns. It fails if the new skill needs the same protected parts or if the method guesses the fragile directions wrong. The formal term is projection: removing or reducing movement in directions believed to be risky.

choose an update that improves the target task while keeping movement in protected directions small

Evidence Check

Report how performance changes as the direction budget changes, tests that remove fragile directions, retained reasoning behavior, task-vector projections, interference between merged models, and comparisons against unconstrained tuning at matched capacity.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when fragile directions are identified incorrectly, when the new task also needs those protected directions, when the direction budget hides necessary learning, or when retained behavior is tested too narrowly.

Connection

DiReCT avoids sensitive data-induced movement. Adam-style optimizer work chooses how updates are measured. CSPO restricts safety movement. RAIN-Merging, FlexRank, and LoRA put the same idea inside adaptation and merging: change only through acceptable directions.

EXEMPLAR 49

Continual VLA, Skill Neologisms, GEPA, And DTO-KD

Continual Updates As Learning New Skills Without Erasing Old Ones

Problem

A deployed system may need to keep learning. But every new lesson can overwrite an old one. For agents and robots, that can look like a lost skill, an unsafe action, or a broken workflow, not just a worse score.

State Space

The state is old behavior, new task data, saved examples for rehearsal, learned control tokens for skills, prompt variants, teacher signals, task signals, and tests on both old and new tasks.

Object

The object is the path of behavior change over time. Think of a worker learning a new procedure without forgetting how to do yesterday's job. The system has to remember old skills and still make room for new ones. Stability means old abilities stay available. Plasticity means the system can still learn new abilities. The stability-plasticity tradeoff is the tension between those two needs.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is prior capability while new behavior is added. The model may change, but it should not cross into forgetting, unsafe drift, or blind imitation of a flawed teacher.

Allowed Movement

The system may replay old examples, add soft control tokens, search prompt variants, or rebalance teacher training. What may change is the new-task behavior and the route used to activate it. What must stay the same is access to old skills when their old conditions appear. Replay means rehearsing old cases so they remain represented. Distillation means learning from a teacher model's outputs or signals.

Why It Works

The method works when old evidence remains in the training loop and new updates are kept local. Replay reminds the model what not to forget. Skill tokens act like labels on tools: use this new behavior only in the setting where it belongs. Searching across prompts preserves alternatives instead of locking onto one brittle instruction. Rebalancing teacher and task signals prevents one source from taking over. The method works only if old skills are tested after the new lesson, not merely assumed to remain.

learn the new task while bounding the loss of old-task behavior and limiting conflict between update signals

Evidence Check

Measure forgetting curves, replay coverage, whether skill tokens stay local, new-task gain, old-task retention, update-signal agreement, prompt diversity, and embodied failures after updates in old and new settings.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when replay misses rare old skills, soft tokens activate outside their valid setting, prompt search overfits a small rollout set, or distillation preserves teacher errors instead of useful knowledge.

Connection

Skill-Pro creates reusable procedures. HDFlow and PACT define physically allowed action steps. RAIN-Merging restricts update directions. Continual VLA, Skill Neologisms, GEPA, and DTO-KD ask how a deployed system can keep changing without losing its identity.

EXEMPLAR 50

Latent Hawkes, OU Identifiability, And Power-Law Discovery

Temporal Data As Asking What Process Made The Pattern

Problem

If one event often comes before another, it is tempting to say the first caused the second. But time order alone can mislead. Both events may be driven by a hidden process, the sampling rate may miss events, or the system may change its rules over time.

State Space

The state is the observed time series or event stream, unseen subprocesses, the rule by which events trigger later events, background drift, measurement noise, possible deliberate changes, and the time scale of observation.

Object

The object is the process rule that created the time pattern. The question is whether the observations narrow the story enough to say which rule made the pattern. Identifiable means the available observations are strong enough to pin down that rule, rather than leaving several different process stories equally possible.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the meaning of the process rule. The inferred relation should describe a stable process, not just a correlation that happened under one sampling schedule.

Allowed Movement

The method may use event paths, averages of time patterns, unseen factors, repeated patterns at different scales, or deliberate changes to the system. It must say which process stories can actually be separated by the observations. The formal terms are latent variable, meaning an unseen factor, spectral feature, meaning a repeated pattern pulled out of a signal, and intervention, meaning a deliberate change used to test what follows.

Why It Works

This works when the time pattern gives more than "one thing often came before another." A doorbell ringing before a dog barks is different from a city siren making many dogs bark; both create order in time, but the underlying story differs. Hawkes-style models ask whether one event temporarily raises the chance of later events. OU-style models ask whether a noisy measurement drifts back toward a usual level. Power-law discovery asks whether a pattern keeps the same shape at small and large scales. What must stay the same is the rule that explains future timing; what may change is the summary used to reveal that rule. The formal term is identifiable: the observed time patterns rule out the rival process stories allowed by the assumptions.

a process claim is supported when observed timing rules out the competing process stories under stated assumptions

Evidence Check

Report sensitivity to time scale, hidden-process stress tests, checks on time-pattern averages, intervention validation, diagnostics for changing rules over time, and whether the inferred process predicts under shifted timing regimes.

Failure Boundary

The method fails when the assumed process family is wrong, hidden drivers make several stories indistinguishable, sampling frequency merges separate events, or the process is treated as stable when it changes over time.

Connection

Distributional Equivalence warns when several causal stories fit the same data. IRNO treats physical dynamics as stable rules. Mamba Markov ICL compresses temporal evidence into state. These temporal-discovery papers ask when time reveals a mechanism rather than only a pattern.

EXEMPLAR 51

FlashWorld And VectorWorld

World Models As Worlds That Change Because Of Actions

Problem

A generated driving scene or game world can look believable one frame at a time and still ignore the action that was taken. If the car turns left, the next state should reflect that turn. For simulation and robotics, pretty video is not enough; the world has to respond lawfully.

State Space
