DecodeShare identifies compact hidden regions at decoding time that can be reused across tasks. Mamba-3 treats richer recurrence as state tracking. LongWriter-Zero separates long-form length, structure, and quality rewards. ThinkV and cache-selection work ask which stored state still changes future reasoning. The formal phrase is a revisable memory state: retained information that affects later decisions and can be updated when new evidence contradicts it.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is future-relevant belief. Old information should be retained only if it can still change the next decision, and wrong old information should be revisable when new evidence contradicts it.

Allowed Movement

What may change is cache compression, sharing hidden regions, using recurrent memory, retrieving external facts, rewarding long structure, or revising assumptions. The method must keep memory connected to current uncertainty, not to raw storage volume.

Why It Works

It works when memory is treated as an updateable belief, not a warehouse. Retrieval matters because a memory that cannot be addressed has no effect. Revision matters because the best belief changes when new evidence arrives. Compact hidden regions matter when they preserve reusable information with less storage. Recurrence matters when it carries enough state across long sequences. Long-writing rewards matter only when they separate length from coherence and structure, otherwise the model optimizes one visible feature while losing the hidden plan. What must stay the same is belief that can affect future decisions; what may change is storage form, retrieval path, or revision.

memory is useful when retained state is enough for future decisions and can be updated when new evidence changes what should be believed

Evidence Check

Test delayed retrieval, contradiction recovery, early-assumption correction, removal of compact hidden regions, recurrent-state stress cases, long-output structure versus content quality, and efficiency measured against successful future decisions rather than storage size.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when retrieval keys encode surface similarity instead of task relevance, when the model cannot revise an early belief, when compression removes rare decisive state, when recurrent memory accumulates hidden error, or when long-output rewards trade structure for unsupported content.

Connection

ThinKV and EntroKV compress context; position-content factorization preserves addressable evidence; temporal explanation asks which past event changed the future state. This group names the agent-side rule: memory is not storage. It is revisable evidence for the next decision.

EXEMPLAR 141

TerminalTraj, ECHO, ThreadWeaver, SCOPE, ThunderAgent, Speculative Actions, And AgentFlow

Making Repeated Work Cheaper Without Skipping The Checks

Problem

An assistant that fixes one file once is different from an assistant that can repeat a checked workflow across many files. Many agent failures are workflow failures: the agent cannot preserve validation, reuse a good procedure, coordinate parallel subtasks, or decide when speculative work is worth the risk.

State Space

The state is task environment, terminal action, file change, validation command, workflow graph, thread, speculative branch, tool latency, reusable skill, verifier result, dependency edge, and rollback point.

Object

The object is a checked work process with costs on each move. It should show which outside action happens first, which later action depends on it, which check can reject a bad state, and what time or tool cost each move carries. TerminalTraj preserves terminal actions and executable validators. ECHO, ThreadWeaver, and SCOPE improve throughput by changing how work is scheduled or reused. ThunderAgent and Speculative Actions treat latency as a graph of work that can be started early and checked later. AgentFlow and related systems turn repeated task structure into reusable process state. The formal phrase is an external process graph with costed transitions: a map of outside actions, dependencies, checks, and costs.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is verified progress through the environment. Faster workflows are useful only if the resulting external state, tests, files, or artifacts still match the user's task goal.

Allowed Movement

What may change is early work on likely branches, parallel work, skill reuse, workflow caching, terminal-task generation, or thread scheduling. The agent must preserve dependency order and keep validators that can reject bad state changes.

Why It Works

It works because agent cost is often in outside actions, not only tokens. A terminal trajectory is valuable when it preserves the action and the check, so the model learns a process rather than a final patch. Speculation helps when branches can be checked cheaply and discarded safely. Threading helps when subtasks are independent enough to overlap. Reuse helps when a verified procedure applies to a new state without solving from scratch. What must stay the same is validator-confirmed progress; what may change is scheduling, reuse, or speculation. Speed only counts when the checks still guard the state changes.

agent throughput helps when cheaper transitions still preserve dependency order and verifier-confirmed external state

Evidence Check

Measure verifier pass rate, wall-clock speedup, rollback frequency, dependency violations, speculative branch waste, workflow reuse accuracy, terminal command fidelity, and whether faster execution changes the final artifact quality.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when speculative actions corrupt state before verification, when parallel threads share hidden dependencies, when reusable skills overfit old environments, when validation is shallow, or when speedups come from skipping evidence-producing steps.

Connection

Skill-Pro treats experience as procedure; ParetoPO prices tool calls; CyberGym and WebDevJudge require executable artifacts. This group gives the workflow-level principle: agent intelligence becomes deployable when reasoning is coupled to cheap, checkable external state transitions.

EXEMPLAR 142

WAVE, TokSuite, ATLAS Multilingual Transfer, Local Redundancy, Information Flow UQ, And DLMR

Retrieving What Answers This Question, Not What Looks Similar

Problem

The same video can answer "who spoke?", "what sound happened?", "when did the door open?", or "was the action unsafe?" A fixed search key may return the generally similar item while missing the evidence needed for the current question.

State Space

The state is user prompt, query intent, text span, audio segment, video clip, language identity, tokenizer unit, retrieved evidence, repeated support, attribution path, and generated answer.

Object

The object is an access key shaped by the task. A useful key is not "find something similar"; it is "find the part that answers this question." WAVE learns prompt-aware audio-video-text representations. TokSuite tests whether tokenizer changes preserve evidence across languages. ATLAS Multilingual Transfer studies scaling across training and evaluation languages. Local Redundancy asks when nearby language evidence is duplicated or still informative. Information Flow UQ and DLMR trace whether retrieved or remembered evidence actually contributed to output. The formal phrase is a task-conditioned access key: a retrieval key defined by the current question, not by generic similarity.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is evidence that answers the user's current question, not generic similarity. If the user asks who spoke, the retrieved evidence should identify the speaker; if the user asks when the door opened, it should preserve timing.

Allowed Movement

What may change is combining audio, video, and text; learning a prompt-shaped search key; normalizing tokenization; removing repeated context; retrieving across languages; or tracing which source the answer used. The method must preserve the evidence route from query to answer.

Why It Works

It works when retrieval is treated as asking a question of the data. The prompt defines which part of an object matters. A video can be relevant by action, speaker, sound, timing, or intent. A multilingual example helps only if tokenization preserves the needed distinction. Removing redundant context helps only when the removed text carries no new evidence for the prompt. Retrieval matters only if the answer actually uses the source. The proof is again a change test: block the retrieved evidence and the answer should lose support or change.

retrieval is valid when selected evidence matches the query intent and changing that evidence changes the generated answer

Evidence Check

Run prompt swaps over the same media, controlled multilingual wording changes, tokenizer robustness checks, repeated-context ablations, retrieval attribution, source-removal tests that ask whether the answer changes when its claimed evidence is removed, and answer changes when the relevant evidence route is blocked.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when prompt-aware representations learn superficial intent labels, when tokenization damage is hidden by English-centric tests, when redundancy measures remove minority-language evidence, or when retrieved evidence is present in context but ignored by generation.

Connection

CRAMF treats retrieval as formal symbol conditioning; provenance-weighted evidence flow tracks memory sources; localized visual evidence responsibility asks which region supports a claim. This group extends the same object across retrieval: access is only useful when the retrieved state is the evidence the current question needs.

EXEMPLAR 143

FeatJND, UniPercept, TTSDS2, DGS-Net, VJA, DAVE, And Visual Attribution Streaming

Measuring The Difference Users Actually Notice Or Rely On

Problem

A generated image can be close pixel by pixel and still look wrong. A voice can match the text and still sound unnatural. A safety mark can be visible to a model but not to a human reviewer. Media quality is about the property users act on, not just numeric closeness to a file.

State Space

The state is generated artifact, reference artifact, human notice threshold, feature representation, audio quality cue, visual intent mark, synthetic-image detector, attribution map, explanation component, and downstream user judgment.

Object

The object is a measurement scale tied to the medium. The ruler should match the failure: what a listener hears, what a viewer notices, what a detector can still use, or which visual region actually caused an answer. FeatJND asks which feature changes become just noticeable after quantization. UniPercept uses multimodal reward signals that better match human perception for text-to-image generation. TTSDS2 builds score-based tests for synthetic speech. DGS-Net preserves transferable clues while detecting generated images. VJA identifies safety instructions embedded in visual marks rather than text. DAVE and Visual Attribution Streaming ask whether explanations point to the visual evidence responsible for an answer. The formal phrase is a perceptual measurement scale: a ruler defined by what users or downstream systems can perceive and rely on.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the property a user or downstream system relies on: quality, authenticity, intent, grounding, or explanation support.

Allowed Movement

What may change is compression, synthesis, detection, guidance, attribution, or evaluation of media. The method must preserve the medium-specific measurement scale rather than replacing it with a convenient substitute score.

Why It Works

It works when evaluation is stated where failure is visible. Just-noticeable changes matter because imperceptible differences and harmful degradations are not the same. Speech evaluation needs sound over time, not only text. Synthetic-image detection needs clues that transfer across generators. Visual jailbreak detection must read the visual instruction surface. Attribution matters when the explanation must locate the part of the artifact that caused the claim. What must stay the same is the property users or downstream systems rely on; what may change is the medium representation or scoring tool.

artifact evaluation is valid when the measurement changes with user-visible or operationally important artifact properties

Evidence Check

Report human agreement, notice thresholds, transfer to new generators, visual-mark attacks, audio quality correlations, region-removal tests for attribution, and cases where pixel, token, or judge metrics disagree with user-visible failure.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when the perceptual model is trained on narrow artifacts, when notice thresholds do not transfer across tasks, when detectors overfit generator artifacts, when visual safety marks are outside the threat model, or when attribution maps are visually plausible but not causal.

Connection

MetamerGen studies different artifacts that feel the same to people; native verifier pages demand artifact-level checking; localized visual evidence pages ask what region supports a claim. This group adds the measurement principle: media quality is not one number until the perceptual or operational ruler is defined.

EXEMPLAR 144

Neural Thickets, LoRA Gradient Descent, LoRA-Pre, GR-LoRA, CLEAR, AutoEP, L2Seg, And PCD

Learning A New Skill Without Wandering Too Far

Problem

A skilled worker can learn a new tool without forgetting the craft. A pretrained model should do the same. The hard question is how far it may move, which small parts should move, and which old behavior must remain fixed.

State Space

The state is the pretrained model settings, nearby model variants, small adapter pieces, training-rule momentum, update component, old-task behavior, new-task goal, stable route segment, search diagnostic, mismatch with deployment data, and retention score.

Object

The object is bounded movement from a useful starting model. The update should be large enough to learn the new skill but small and directed enough that old skills do not drift. Neural Thickets describes many nearby task-specialist models around a pretrained point. LoRA Gradient Descent analyzes the small low-rank update itself, meaning an update forced to use a small number of directions. LoRA-Pre compresses optimizer state through low-rank online regression. GR-LoRA separates updates that protect old skills from updates that learn new ones. CLEAR and related methods preserve base capability while moving small modules. AutoEP changes optimizer behavior from live evidence about the search landscape. L2Seg reuses stable route segments so solvers spend effort on uncertain parts. PCD searches for novel high-performing samples beyond a static dataset. The formal phrase is bounded local movement: the update is allowed to move only through directions that improve the new task without large base-behavior drift.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is base capability plus task-relevant movement. The update should reach the new behavior while keeping old behavior, stable segments, and reusable structure intact.

Allowed Movement

What may change is small-direction update factors, selected modules, separated update components, compressed optimizer state, search parameters, or explored samples. The method must bound destructive movement through fragile directions.

Why It Works

It works when pretraining has already placed useful skills nearby. A small adapter helps if the new task can be reached through a small change. Separating update directions helps if old-task and new-task movements can be distinguished. Optimizer-state compression helps if momentum mostly lives in a predictable small-direction pattern. Landscape feedback helps when the search rule should change after observing rough or smooth regions. Stable-segment reuse helps because known-safe parts can be compressed and effort can focus on unresolved decisions. What must stay the same is base behavior; what may change is the smallest directed movement that buys the new skill.

adaptation is valid when the update improves the new task while bounding drift in old behavior

Evidence Check

Measure old-task retention, new-task gain, sensitivity to adapter size, conflict between old-task and new-task updates, error from compressing the training rule's memory, density of nearby useful model variants, module-selection ablations, deployment mismatch, and whether stable segments stay stable after reuse.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when the new task is not near the pretrained model, when small adapters cannot represent the needed change, when update separation misclassifies useful conflict, when compressed momentum loses rare directions, or when stable segments change after surrounding context changes.

Connection

Federated personalization handles shared-private updates; optimizer path rules explain movement; privacy work asks where private traces live in updates. This group gives the adaptation principle: efficient updating works only when the allowed movement matches the desired behavior change.

EXEMPLAR 145

Efficient Diffusion Via Landing, PCD, NEXCO, ReaSyn, Rex, Quantized Consistency Docking, And DCFold

Generating Objects That Pass The Rules They Must Obey

Problem

A bridge design is not good because it resembles old bridges. It is good if it stands, fits the site, and meets the budget. Generated scientific objects have the same issue: chemical routes must be valid, designs must hit the intended goals, samples must satisfy constraints, and folded or docked structures must pass physical checks.

State Space

Imagine designing a bridge, medicine, or folded molecule with a checklist on the table. Some boxes are absolute: this part must equal that value, this route must be makeable, this structure must fit. Other boxes are tradeoffs: stronger but heavier, better binding but harder synthesis, faster solver but less exact checking. The state is the candidate being built, the rules it must exactly satisfy, the limits it must stay within, the competing scores being balanced, hidden pieces to fill in, edits to the making route, solver mistakes, docking or folding checks, and the receipt showing whether the candidate is allowed.

Object

The object is search over allowed objects. The generator is not free to make anything that looks likely; each partial move should stay near objects that obey the hard rules or move closer to them. Efficient Diffusion via Landing steers samples toward hard allowed regions without expensive projection at every step. PCD treats design with several goals at once as sampling under target tradeoffs. NEXCO makes solution expansion a search over many allowed combinations, where each partial choice limits what can come next. ReaSyn edits full synthesis routes through insertion, deletion, and substitution. Rex uses numerical inversion for high-precision reconstruction and Boltzmann sampling, where better but costly states are sampled with controlled randomness. Quantized Consistency Docking and DCFold-style systems make repeated scientific inner-loop calls cheap enough for search. The formal phrase is feasible-set search process: generation is guided by the set of objects the domain rules allow.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is rule-passing possibility. A candidate should not merely resemble old data; it should still satisfy the equalities, limits, route rules, physical checks, or solver tests that make it usable in the domain.

Allowed Movement

What may change is removing noise from a rough candidate, filling hidden pieces, editing a synthesis route, asking for a different compromise among goals, reversing a numerical process, or making an inner checker faster. Each movement should either reduce a rule violation or keep a path open toward an allowed object.

Why It Works

It works when generation is treated as search with allowed moves. Landing helps if the sample is guided toward the allowed region without paying for a full repair at every step. Tradeoff-guided sampling helps if the system can ask for different compromises and still stay inside the rules. Masked expansion helps if each partial solution leaves legal next choices. Route editing helps because a molecule is useful only if it can be made. Solver acceleration matters because scientific design needs many checks, not one polished sample. What must stay the same is rule-passing possibility; what may change is the route used to reach a useful candidate. The key test is whether the path stays close enough to allowed objects that the final candidate can pass the domain checks.

a generated object is acceptable when the search path reaches the target region while keeping rule violations below the domain threshold

Evidence Check

Report rule violation, tradeoff coverage, route validity, edit distance to feasible routes, solver-call budget, docking or folding verification, guidance ablations, and failures where plausible samples violate domain constraints.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when constraints are incomplete, when guidance optimizes a substitute score that breaks physical validity, when tradeoff conditioning misses sparse allowed regions, when route edits create impossible intermediates, or when accelerated solvers introduce biased feasibility checks.

Connection

Molecule-route exemplars treat generation as nested constraints; path-accurate sampler pages measure distribution movement; scientific workflow pages demand executable verification. This group states the domain-level rule: generation becomes design only when feasibility is part of the state space.

EXEMPLAR 146

CMRU, LLapDiff, ConFlux, Rare-Update Bandits, FlashRNN, CONTINUUM, And Analog-Friendly Recurrent Systems

Keeping The Right State When Time Is Irregular And Updates Cost Money

Problem

A weather station, sensor, or wearable does not always report in neat equal steps. Some updates are expensive, some signals remember their past, and some devices have tiny energy budgets. A time-series model fails if it treats time as evenly spaced text and treats hardware as an afterthought.

State Space

Imagine a nurse watching a patient whose readings arrive at uneven times. A two-minute gap and a two-hour gap mean different things, and a tiny wearable cannot recompute a full chart after every pulse. The state is the timestamp, the unseen condition that may be changing between readings, how memory fades with time, the order of variables, nearby sensor channels, the small note the model keeps, the loop that updates that note, analog or low-precision memory, the expensive design summary used by a bandit method, rare update decisions, and the energy budget.

Object

The object is a state update rule that respects time and machine constraints. The rule must decide what to remember, how that memory fades or changes with real timestamps, and when an expensive update is worth doing. CMRU preserves small-number states, history effects, and noise resilience for ultra-low-power recurrence. LLapDiff models hidden paths through time so irregular timestamps can be evaluated directly. ConFlux reorganizes variables and nearby channels for many-signal foundation models. Rare-Update Bandits reduce expensive design updates while controlling missed-opportunity cost. FlashRNN and CONTINUUM-style systems make recurrent or stateful execution respect machine constraints. The formal phrase is constrained temporal state dynamics: future-useful state must survive irregular time, noise, rounded numbers, update cost, and hardware limits.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the future-useful memory. The model should keep the facts about timing, trend, and recent history that change the next forecast or decision, even when updates are sparse, low precision, noisy, or energy-limited.

Allowed Movement

What may change is storing a rounded small state, accumulating several updates before applying them, choosing a different way to encode time, reordering variables, updating only rarely, or using specialized machine kernels. The method must keep the time-change rule needed for future prediction. The formal phrase temporal dynamics means the rule for how the saved state changes as real time passes.

Why It Works

It works by matching memory to the real process. History effects matter when the current observation is not enough. Irregular-time representations help when timestamps are real information, not missing data. Cross-variable patching helps when variables have local organization. Rare updates help when recomputing expensive design information every step is unnecessary. Hardware-aware recurrence helps when deployment decides which state transitions are feasible. What must stay the same is the state information needed for future forecasts; what may change is when and how the system updates it under real machine limits.

a temporal model is valid when its state update preserves forecast-relevant information under timestamp, noise, quantization, and update-cost constraints

Evidence Check

Evaluate irregular-time forecasts, tests that remove cross-variable links, small-number and history-effect stress tests, noise robustness, energy or memory traces, missed-opportunity curves from rare updates, and long-horizon drift under hardware-shaped recurrence.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when the time representation cannot handle abrupt regime changes, when variable ordering is dataset-specific, when quantized memory loses rare transitions, when rare updates miss sudden shifts, or when hardware savings remove the state needed for prediction.

Connection

Temporal explanation asks which history affects future state; machine-coupled dynamics makes hardware part of the solver; memory revision treats retained state as future evidence. This group gives the time-series version: useful state is not whatever fits in memory, but what preserves the dynamics under real sampling and machine constraints.

EXEMPLAR 147

Nash Equilibria With Coupling Constraints, Structured Stackelberg Games, RQE Actor-Critic, RSPG, NonZero, First-Price Auctions, MASPOB, LatentMAS, And Value Of Variance

Keeping Several Agents From Breaking Each Other

Problem

If several people share one kitchen, each person's plan changes what the others can do. Opening the oven, using the sink, or taking the last ingredient can make another plan impossible. Multi-agent systems have the same problem: each agent's update changes the world the others are learning in.

State Space

The state is the agents' joint plans, shared allowed region, player type, leader action, follower response, payoff values, communication graph, hidden message, disagreement level, local change, auction bid, and learning process.

Object

Imagine several people cooking in one kitchen. One person taking the last pan changes what everyone else can cook. The object is the joint state where every agent's move is judged after the others can react. What must stay the same is joint validity: the shared rules are not broken, and no agent's improvement only works by making another agent's move impossible. What may change is each action rule, bid, message, prompt, or local change. Nash equilibria with coupling constraints ask whether shared constraints create a valid joint allowed set. Structured Stackelberg Games learn leader action rules against follower types. RQE Actor-Critic studies risk-aware systematic responses in general-sum games. RSPG handles games where each agent sees only part of a large population. NonZero checks local changes without listing every joint action. First-Price Auctions study bidding under learning. MASPOB optimizes prompts across a workflow. LatentMAS shares hidden state between agents. Value of Variance uses disagreement to detect debate collapse. The formal phrase is constrained equilibrium: a stable joint state inside the shared rules.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is stable joint improvement. Each agent's update should remain meaningful under the others' possible responses and under the shared constraints of the system.

Allowed Movement

What may change is action rules, prompts, messages, bids, local changes, or penalties for premature agreement. The movement must respect the joint allowed set and the model of how others respond.

Why It Works

It works when reaction is part of the state. A shared constraint matters because one agent can make another's action invalid. Leader-follower structure helps when those roles are real and the follower response is modeled, not guessed. Population summaries help when many agents can be approximated by the crowd state without erasing important minorities. Debate variance helps because independent evidence should not vanish too early. Hidden communication helps only if it carries useful information without hiding errors from checks. What must stay the same is joint validity; what may change is each agent's action rule, bid, message, or prompt.

a multi-agent update is valid when joint movement stays inside shared rules and becomes stable under modeled responses

Evidence Check

Report rule violations, whether any agent can improve by exploiting the others, follower-response missed-opportunity cost, response stability, population-summary error, local-change coverage, auction revenue under learning, debate agreement collapse, and communication tests for text versus hidden state.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when agents have unmodeled incentives, when shared constraints have ignored hard regions, when hidden communication hides errors from verifiers, when variance rewards empty disagreement, or when local-deviation checks miss coordinated failures.

Connection

OMAC treats collaboration architecture as a surface where changing one rule changes the whole system's behavior; ParetoPO prices tool use; preference-training rules turn human evidence into decision rules. This group gives the multi-agent principle: a deployed agent system is a coupled game, not a collection of independent model calls.

EXEMPLAR 148

CounselBench, Copyright-Bench, SecFid, Legal Agents, DR Tulu, Seizure-Semiology-Suite, And MADQA

Counting Dangerous Mistakes More Than Ordinary Mistakes

Problem

In counseling, law, security, and medicine, two wrong answers can have very different costs. A fluent answer that gives unsafe advice can be worse than a refusal. A clinically plausible report without evidence can be worse than saying uncertainty remains.

State Space

The state is domain rule, user pressure, safety constraint, expert rubric, legal permission, harmful advice, benign refusal, clinical event, evidence access, answerability by an ideal expert, and downstream harm.

Object

Imagine grading two driving mistakes: parking slightly crooked and speeding through a school crossing. Both are wrong, but they should not receive the same penalty. The object is a scoring rule where errors are counted by the harm they can cause. What must stay the same is domain-valid action: the answer should satisfy the task while respecting safety, law, evidence, and professional boundaries. What may change is the probe, rubric, refusal rule, evidence requirement, or legal check. CounselBench measures counseling responses under expert and adversarial risk. Copyright-Bench evaluates lawful source choices in commercial tasks. SecFid evaluates defenses through both security and benign-use cost. Legal-agent work treats law as a boundary on allowed action. DR Tulu uses research rubrics that evolve as model behavior changes. Seizure-Semiology-Suite checks clinical timing. MADQA separates answerability, evidence access, and document reasoning. The formal phrase is asymmetric-loss measurement rule: mistakes are weighted by the harm they cause.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is domain-valid action: the system must satisfy the task while respecting the risk and allowed-action constraints that define the domain.

Allowed Movement

What may change is adversarial probing, rubric factoring, evidence citation requirements, legal boundary checks, refusal-cost measurement, or separation of answer access from model reasoning. The evaluator must keep the domain's error costs explicit.

Why It Works

It works because high-stakes evaluation is a decision problem, not a correctness poll. The metric must encode what happens if the model is wrong in a specific way. Expert rubrics matter when generic judges reward fluent unsafe answers. Legal provenance matters because the same output can be allowed or forbidden depending on source rights. Security tradeoffs matter because a blocker that destroys benign use is not deployable. Clinical timing matters because the explanation must match the observed medical sequence. What must stay the same is domain-valid action under risk; what may change is how the evaluator probes, factors, or weights the mistake.

a domain score is valid when errors are weighted by harm, allowed actions, evidence support, and benign-use cost

Evidence Check

Report expert disagreement, adversarial prompt sensitivity, lawful-source decisions, refusal false positives, security-versus-benign-use tradeoffs, clinical timing, expert answerability, evidence-access ablations, and cases where generic judges overrate unsafe outputs.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when rubrics miss the real harm, when legal labels are ambiguous, when expert evaluation is too sparse, when adversarial cases become template-like, when security cost is ignored, or when evidence access and reasoning ability are conflated.

Connection

Operational tail-risk control measures rare high-cost events; expert measurement decomposition asks what experts observe; legal feasibility pages define action boundaries. This group names the broader rule: high-stakes evaluation must measure loss where harm actually lives.

EXEMPLAR 149

Common Corpus, Hubble, OpenDataArena, Data Governance And Curation, Source Screening, Sequential Data Values, And Multi-Epoch Scaling

Treating The Dataset Like Part Of The Experiment

Problem

A cooking result cannot be reproduced if no one knows the ingredients, where they came from, or how many times they were reused. Model claims have the same problem. If the data is hidden, legally unclear, contaminated, or impossible to audit, the claim about what the model learned is weak.

State Space

The state is document source, license, language, code fragment, inserted sensitive text, training overlap, filter rule, curation decision, source value, data reuse count, feature extractor, and downstream capability claim.

Object

Imagine trying to reproduce a recipe without knowing the ingredients, the store, substitutions, or how many times leftovers were reused. The result cannot be audited. The object is a data process with receipts. What must stay the same is traceable training evidence: where examples came from, what rights they had, what was filtered out, where probes were inserted, how sources were weighted, and how often data was reused. What may change is filtering, deduplication, license screening, source selection, controlled probe insertion, or reuse. Common Corpus makes lawful, provenance-documented pretraining data a research artifact. Hubble releases model pairs with known sensitive-text insertions for memorization measurement. OpenDataArena treats open training data as a reproducible public artifact with downstream value. Source Screening studies source-level selection. Sequential Data Values estimates data value based on what has already been selected. Multi-Epoch Scaling separates useful reuse from repeated exposure that changes scaling behavior. The formal phrase is auditable data-generating and data-curation process: the data source, filtering, rights, probes, and reuse are visible enough to support the claim.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is traceable evidence about where training signal came from, how it was filtered, how often it was reused, and which downstream claim it supports.

Allowed Movement

The pipeline may filter, deduplicate, license-screen, insert controlled probes, weight sources, reuse data, or select examples, but every movement should remain auditable enough to support claims about legality, memorization, representation, and value.

Why It Works

It works by moving the scientific object upstream. If the corpus is documented, model behavior can be connected to known sources. Controlled insertions make memorization measurable because the evaluator knows the sensitive source. Source-level screening helps because examples arrive through sources with different distributions and rights. Sequential value matters because one example's contribution depends on what the model has already seen. Reuse analysis matters because more passes over the same data are not the same as more data. What must stay the same is traceable training evidence; what may change is filtering, weighting, probing, or reuse.

model evidence is reproducible when sources, rights, filters, probes, and reuse counts are inspectable for the claimed behavior

Evidence Check

Report license coverage, provenance fields, source distributions, filter effects, memorization probes with known insertions, downstream value by source, reuse-rate curves, contamination audits, and whether another lab can reconstruct the data condition behind the claim.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when provenance is incomplete, when lawful status is uncertain, when filtering removes underrepresented language, when controlled insertions do not match real private exposure, when data value is measured after confounded training choices, or when reuse effects are mistaken for new information.

Connection

Data path credit asks how examples move parameters; privacy geometry asks what individual traces leak; prompt-conditioned retrieval asks whether evidence is accessed later. This group states the data-level first principle: the training substrate is part of the experiment, not a hidden precondition.

EXEMPLAR 150

VALUEFLOW, Ranking Time Series, AGREE, Accuracy Auctions, Linguistic Nepotism, And Value Of Variance

Reading Human Scores As Noisy Evidence, Not Final Truth

Problem

Two reviewers can use the same score for different reasons. One may value beauty, another clarity, another safety, another cultural fit. Human labels are evidence about value, but they are noisy, strategic, time-dependent, and sometimes biased by language or group identity.

State Space

The state is hidden value, annotator, criterion, pairwise comparison, time-varying rank, aesthetic dimension, private value, language identity, citation preference, debate disagreement, and aggregate decision.

Object

Imagine asking five people to rate a meal. One cares about spice, one about presentation, one about allergies, one about price, and one about tradition. The scores are useful, but none is the value itself. The object is a model of what human scores are evidence for. What must stay the same is the decision-relevant value after noise, disagreement, incentives, time changes, and language or group bias are accounted for. What may change is ranking, aggregation, auction design, dimension splitting, or disagreement analysis. VALUEFLOW measures subjective constructs through ranking-based value flow. Ranking Time Series handles noisy pairwise ranking over time. AGREE splits aesthetic judgments into multiple dimensions. Accuracy Auctions models correctness when agents value it differently. Linguistic Nepotism studies language-based citation preference rather than evidence relevance. Value of Variance treats disagreement across agents as a signal for collective reasoning. In plain terms, the real value is hidden and labels are imperfect observations of it.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the value signal after accounting for noise, disagreement, strategic reporting, time variation, and group or language bias.

Allowed Movement

The method may rank, aggregate, auction, split dimensions, penalize premature agreement, or correct for bias, but it must not flatten disagreement into one number unless that number preserves the decision-relevant value.

Why It Works

It works when labels are treated as observations, not as the value itself. Pairwise ranking can be more stable than absolute scores when annotators use scales differently. Time-series ranking helps when preferences drift. Multi-dimensional scoring helps when an average hides why an artifact is good or bad. Auctions help when private values determine the social utility of correctness. Bias analysis helps when language or group identity changes evaluation independent of evidence. Disagreement helps when premature agreement hides weak reasoning. What must stay the same is decision-relevant value after noise and bias are accounted for; what may change is how reports are ranked, split, aggregated, or priced.

a human-score model is valid when labels reveal the target value after accounting for noise, criteria, incentives, time, and bias

Evidence Check

Report inter-annotator variance, dimension reliability, pairwise-ranking consistency, time drift, incentive compatibility, tests where language identity is changed while the evidence is held fixed, variance collapse, and decisions that change when subjective dimensions are kept separate.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when the hidden value cannot be recovered from labels, when ranking loses absolute safety thresholds, when auction incentives are misspecified, when dimensions are chosen after seeing results, or when bias correction removes real domain differences.

Connection

Preference-training rules treat losses as decision rules; perceptual measurement scales define user-visible artifact quality; high-stakes evaluation encodes asymmetric harm. This group supplies the human-measurement layer: before training against value, the system has to say what kind of evidence a human score actually is.

EXEMPLAR 151

TRACE Effort, CE-Graph, T3, Reasoning With Sampling, RAGEN-2, And OpenThoughts

Checking Whether The Trace Did Real Work

Problem

A model can pass a verifier with a trace that looks like reasoning but carries little work. The final answer may be right, the explanation may be long, and the format may satisfy the rubric, while the actual search, checking, or dependency resolution is shallow.

State Space

The state is problem instance, partial trace, verifier signal, shortened prefix, dependency graph, hidden effort, sampled branch, answer, shortcut feature, and task-specific information.

Object

The object is evidence that the intermediate process mattered. A trace should contain work the answer depends on, not just words that look like work. TRACE Effort asks whether verifier success appears before enough reasoning has occurred. CE-Graph treats reasoning as graph-shaped dependencies rather than a flat chain. T3 and Reasoning with Sampling make hidden thinking or sampled branches part of compute allocation. RAGEN-2 checks whether traces still depend on inputs. OpenThoughts supplies explicit trace data whose value depends on whether intermediate steps carry recoverable task information. The formal phrase is process-evidence channel: the trace is useful only if changing it changes the later answer or verification result for the right reason.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is task-specific work: the trace should contain information that changes the later answer for the right reason, not only text that resembles work.

Allowed Movement

The method may shorten traces, make controlled changes to prefixes, sample branches, build dependency graphs, train on traces, or allocate hidden thinking compute, but the movement must test whether the intermediate state is causally useful for solving the instance.

Why It Works

It works because reasoning is not directly visible. We infer it by changing the process. If a verifier score stays high after most of the trace is removed, the trace was not necessary evidence. If changing a dependency node changes the answer predictably, the graph carried structure. If sampled thoughts help only when they explore distinct states, sampling is useful compute; if they collapse to paraphrases, it is decoration. What must stay the same is task-specific work; what may change is the trace prefix, branch, graph edge, or hidden compute. The trace matters only when the later answer actually depends on it.

a trace is evidence of reasoning only when changing task-relevant trace state changes the answer or verifier outcome through a valid dependency path

Evidence Check

Report prefix-shortening curves, whether traces still depend on the input, dependency-scramble tests, branch diversity, answer changes under trace edits, verifier success before and after removing work, and examples where polished traces pass while short-circuiting the real problem.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when traces are post-hoc explanations, when the verifier rewards style, when sampled branches are correlated copies, when graph dependencies are imposed after the fact, or when truncation destroys language fluency rather than reasoning state.

Connection

RAGEN-2 names input-dependent collapse; Tell-Tale Norm measures internal compute intensity; h1 expands dependency horizon. This group gives the test that connects them: a reasoning trace matters only if it carries process evidence that later computation actually uses.

EXEMPLAR 152

Train-before-Test, Prescriptive Scaling, CALIPER, CV Relative Instability, Benchmarking At The Edge Of Comprehension, And Finite Test Certification

Stating The Recipe Behind The Benchmark Score

Problem

A student's score means different things if they saw practice problems, used a calculator, had extra time, or took a different version of the test. A model benchmark score is the same. It depends on prompt format, allowed adaptation, model version, sampling rule, evaluator noise, and task familiarity.

State Space

The state is base model, adaptation data, benchmark item, prompt protocol, fine-tuning rule, scoring rule, model version, item population, uncertainty interval, and reported capability claim.

Object

Imagine reporting a student's score without saying whether they used notes, had extra time, saw practice questions, or took a harder version. The number cannot be compared fairly. The object is capability with the test recipe attached. What must stay the same is the meaning of the claim: immediate behavior, adapted potential, scaffolded behavior, a skill that still holds when prompts, item samples, scaffolds, or judges change, or a finite-sample estimate over a named item population. What may change is adaptation, scaffolds, prompt format, item sampling, judge, model version, or uncertainty estimate, but those choices must be part of the score. Train-before-Test measures performance after matched benchmark-specific adaptation. Prescriptive Scaling asks what evaluation protocol should guide a future deployment decision. CALIPER and CV Relative Instability expose when measured rankings change under protocol or sampling variation. Benchmarking at the Edge of Comprehension studies local verification at capability boundaries. Finite Test Certification asks what finite evidence can certify. The formal phrase is protocol-conditioned capability functional: a score that depends on the model plus the evaluation recipe, not the model alone.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the meaning of the capability claim: the score should say whether it measures immediate behavior, adapted potential, scaffolded behavior, a skill that still holds when prompts, item samples, scaffolds, or judges change, or a finite-sample estimate under a named item population.

Allowed Movement

The evaluator may adapt the model, change prompts, choose items, estimate uncertainty, or prescribe a protocol for decision making, but it must name the protocol as part of the measured object.

Why It Works

It works by refusing to treat a score as intrinsic. The model is only one part of the measurement. Matched adaptation can reveal potential that direct scoring hides. Prescriptive protocols help when the user cares about a future use case rather than a static leaderboard. Instability measures help because rankings move under finite samples and noisy judges. Finite certification helps only when the sampled population and allowed system changes are fixed. What must stay the same is the meaning of the capability claim; what may change is the protocol, but only if the report names it.

reported capability = result of model, adaptation rule, prompt protocol, item population, scorer, and system identity together

Evidence Check

Report direct versus adapted rankings, protocol sensitivity, prompt sensitivity, item resampling intervals, whether judge confidence matches correctness, model-version locks, finite-sample confidence, and cases where a leaderboard order reverses after matched adaptation or system changes.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when adaptation leaks test answers, when the protocol is chosen after seeing results, when the item population is too narrow for the claim, when model versions drift, or when uncertainty is reported for sampling noise while ignoring prompt and judge variation.

Connection

ATLAS treats evaluation as adaptive measurement; Micro-Benchmarking Reliability asks whether ranks are stable; agent gyms ask which scaffold is being measured. This group names the larger principle: capability is always measured through a protocol, so the protocol is part of the claim being made.

EXEMPLAR 153

Omni-Reward, JudgeBench, RLVepsR, VISUALSWAP, FlowGuard, DeceptionDecoded, And Weak-Strong Verification

Checking A Score After The Model Learns To Game It

Problem

A shortcut score can look useful before anyone optimizes against it. Once a model learns to chase that score, it searches for loopholes. The question is whether the score still ranks behavior correctly after the policy adapts to its weaknesses.

State Space

Imagine a classroom where students learn exactly what the grader rewards. At first the grade may be a useful clue about learning. After students train against it, the state includes the real behavior we want, the shortcut score, the judge's output, the model's menu of likely behaviors, training pressure, image/video/text/tool artifacts, checker labels, adversarial inputs, weaker and stronger checkers, and the gap between the score and the real target. The formal phrase policy distribution means the menu of likely model behaviors after training.

Object

Imagine using a bathroom scale to judge health, then paying people to maximize the number on that scale. Before payment, the scale may be a useful clue. After payment, people can game it. The object is a score after the model is trained to chase it. Omni-Reward, JudgeBench, RLVepsR, VISUALSWAP, FlowGuard, DeceptionDecoded, and Weak-Strong Verification all ask whether the score still tracks the real target once it becomes something the model actively searches against. The formal phrase is optimized measurement channel: a measurement path that has become a training target.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is the real ranking after training pressure. If artifact A is truly better or safer than artifact B, the shortcut score should still put A ahead of B even after the model learns the score's weaknesses.

Allowed Movement

The system may train reward models, combine judges, use cheap checkers before expensive ones, train behavior toward high scores, or test image, video, text, and tool artifacts. But it must audit the measuring path after that path becomes the thing the model is trying to win. The formal term objective means the score or rule the training process is trying to increase.

Why It Works

It works only when the ruler and the training pressure are checked together. A substitute score is not just a noisy observation; it becomes the hill the model climbs. If score mistakes are scattered and small, training can still help. If the mistakes have a loophole, training searches for that loophole and makes it larger. Multimodal rewards add another risk: the score may read text while the real failure lives in the image, video, or tool artifact. Weak verification can work when weak labels keep enough of the true ranking in the region the stronger model explores. What must stay the same is the real target order after the model learns to chase the score.

a substitute score is safe to optimize when its best-scoring outputs remain in a region where score order agrees with target order

Evidence Check

Report disagreement before and after training against the score, adversarial reward probes, judge disagreement, multimodal evidence swaps, whether weak-checker confidence matches stronger checking, reward hacking examples, and audits of artifacts selected by the substitute score but rejected by domain or native verifiers.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when shortcut-score mistakes follow a pattern the model can exploit, when training pushes behavior into cases the judge never learned to grade, when the reward reads the wrong evidence channel, when weak labels become too confident, or when the real target itself was never specified clearly enough.

Connection

RLVepsR and verifier triage treat labels as noisy update signals; high-stakes evaluation weights errors by harm; artifact-native verification checks the object itself. This group states the substitute-score principle: a score is not reliable because it correlates before training; it must stay aligned under pressure.

EXEMPLAR 154

Midtraining Bridges, LR Decay, Local Redundancy, BLL-Loss, LALP, OpenThoughts, Sequential Data Values, And DiReCT

Using Data While The Model Can Still Learn From It

Problem

A lesson has different value on the first day, the week before an exam, and after the exam is over. Training data works the same way. The same examples can teach a capability, become redundant, interfere with old behavior, or arrive too late to move the model without damage.

State Space

The state is the model's current knob settings, the trainer's memory of earlier pushes, learning rate, data mixture, training phase boundary, update direction, nearby repeated evidence, learning window, selected example, retained skill, and target data source.

Object

The object is a training path where data value depends on time. The same example can be powerful early, redundant later, or harmful after the model has already settled into fragile habits. Midtraining Bridges places specialized data between pretraining and posttraining so data-source movement is gradual. LR Decay studies how learning-rate timing changes whether data still moves the model's settings. Local Redundancy measures whether nearby update families leave room for specialization. BLL-Loss and LALP shape language-learning pressure. OpenThoughts supplies reasoning traces whose value depends on phase. Sequential Data Values and DiReCT estimate data value based on the current model state and how hard each direction is to move. The formal phrase is time-indexed parameter path: examples act on the model at a particular training moment, so their effect depends on where the model already is.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is useful movement: data should move parameters toward target behavior without erasing needed capabilities or spending update budget after the learning window has closed.

Allowed Movement

The trainer may change mixture weights, shrink learning rates, insert bridge data, select examples, add trace data, or avoid hard-to-move directions, but the movement must be evaluated relative to the current point on the training path.

Why It Works

It works because training is path-dependent. Updates act on the model that exists now, not on a blank model. Early data can shape features; late data may only tune behavior or cause brittle shifts. Bridge data helps when it keeps successive updates compatible. Redundancy helps when several nearby examples teach the same skill without damaging old ones. Direction-aware selection helps because some directions are cheap to move while others destroy established structure. What must stay the same is useful movement without erasing retained skill; what may change is which data arrives at which moment.

an example is valuable at a training moment when its target gain is larger than interference, movement cost, repeated evidence, provenance cost, and lost retained capability

Evidence Check

Report what changes when a training phase is removed, mixture-weight sweeps, learning-rate timing, representation drift, retained-capability tests, whether each training example keeps pushing toward the later target at that stage of training, movement cost, repeated-evidence measures, and examples where adding the same data earlier or later changes the result.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when a bridge distribution is only superficially close, when late data is too weak to move useful features, when early data overwrites broad capability, when redundancy is measured in the wrong neighborhood, or when selected data improves validation while damaging unmeasured skills.

Connection

DiReCT treats data as control over directions that are easier or harder to move; Common Corpus makes source provenance explicit; h1 uses curriculum for horizon length. This group gives the training first principle: examples are interventions on a timed path, so their value is conditional, not intrinsic.

EXEMPLAR 155

Stable Video Infinity, SANA-Video, Flex-Forcing, MotionStream, LiveMoments, FlashWorld, VectorWorld, And dWorldEval

Keeping A Video In The Same World As Time Passes

Problem

A long video can look good one frame at a time and still fail as a story. A person's face can drift, an object can vanish, motion can lose its cause, or small generation errors can pile up until the future no longer belongs to the same world.

State Space

The state is frame, hidden world state, time index, object identity, camera path, action token, chunk boundary, denoising step, self-generated error, key-frame anchor, streaming memory, and physical consistency rule.

Object

The object is a video generator that keeps returning to the same world while it invents the next moment. Think of filming a room while walking through it: the chair can move in view, the camera can turn, and the lighting can shift, but it is still the same chair in the same room unless the story gives a reason for change. Each new frame depends on earlier generated frames, so the model has to remember identity, motion cause, scene layout, and the small mistakes it already made. Stable Video Infinity trains on errors the model itself creates so it can recover when those errors appear at deployment. SANA-Video uses constant memory for high-resolution long video. Flex-Forcing connects future-aware and streaming chunks. MotionStream teaches a streaming student from a stronger teacher that can see both past and future. LiveMoments anchors restoration with an original high-quality key photo. FlashWorld, VectorWorld, and dWorldEval evaluate videos through actions and world state. The formal phrase is error-corrected trajectory generator: a generator whose repeated next-step moves preserve one world while correcting drift.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is world continuity: the generated sequence should keep the same objects, relations, motion causes, and recoverable state across time.

Allowed Movement

What may change is denoising, chunking, streaming, recycling errors, anchoring on key frames, or using action-conditioned world state. Each movement must preserve the state variables future frames depend on.

Why It Works

It works when video is treated as one changing world, not a stack of attractive still pictures. A long generator makes thousands of small guesses. If each guess is judged only by whether the next frame looks good, tiny mistakes become facts for later frames, and the world slowly changes into a different world. Training on model-made errors helps because the model practices recovering from the kind of wrong turns it will actually create. Constant memory helps only when it keeps the few facts later frames still need, such as who is present, where objects are, and what motion is already underway. Future-aware teachers help streaming students because the teacher can show which short-term details matter for the future. Key-frame anchors help when a trusted photo must keep texture and identity from drifting. World simulators help when a pretty frame is not enough because the next action must have the consequence that the same world would produce.

long video is valid when repeated transitions preserve identity, scene structure, action effects, and bounded error over time

Evidence Check

Report identity persistence, object-track survival, long-horizon drift, camera and motion consistency, error-recovery tests, chunk-boundary artifacts, key-frame dependence, action-change consistency, and evaluation that separates plausible frames from coherent paths through one world.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when correction learns cosmetic repair but not state recovery, when constant memory drops rare decisive variables, when streaming distillation loses long-range constraints, or when a video benchmark can be passed by frame realism without physical continuity.

Connection

VideoKR treats long video as retained evidence; ThinKV treats context as sufficient state; IRNO studies accumulated physical error. This group gives the generative version: the problem is not producing more frames, but keeping one coherent world through repeated approximate transitions.

EXEMPLAR 156

Chamaileon, Complexa, TD3B, mCLM, FIDIA, Protein Autoregressive Modeling, TEDBench/MiAE, And FLIP2

Designing Biology By Keeping Sequence, Shape, Target, And Making Route Together

Problem

A protein or molecule is not useful just because it looks likely. It has to fold, bind the right target, avoid the wrong state, be makeable, and improve the selected property. If those conditions are optimized separately, the final candidate can fail in the lab.

State Space

The state is sequence, backbone, atom-level assembly, target context, binder pose, reaction-compatible building block, transition direction, candidate batch, fitness label, benchmark-neighbor cluster, and experimental or structural rule.

Object

The object is a biological candidate whose required roles are tied together. A useful binder is not just a string, and it is not just a shape. It is a string that folds into a shape, reaches a target in the right pose, can be made from available chemistry, and is chosen because it improves the property the experiment cares about. If those pieces are optimized separately, the final candidate can look good on paper while failing in the lab. Chamaileon models sequence and structure across target contexts. Complexa learns binder-target evidence from synthetic and experimental assemblies. TD3B optimizes binders that push a desired state transition. mCLM uses makeable functional building blocks with language descriptions. FIDIA optimizes inverse-folding choices for selecting the best candidate from a batch. Protein autoregressive modeling generates backbones while respecting part-whole structure. TEDBench/MiAE and FLIP2 test whether protein claims survive near-copy examples and testing away from familiar examples. The formal phrase is conditional biological complex: the single joined object made by sequence, structure, target, making route, and selection goal.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is lab-relevant feasibility: sequence, structure, target context, makeability, and selection rule should agree on the same candidate.

Allowed Movement

What may change is sequence, backbone, assembly, building block, binder pose, transition direction, or candidate batch. The method must keep the required biological conditions coupled instead of optimizing each artifact independently.

Why It Works

It works when the model keeps the lab question whole. Changing a sequence changes the fold it can make. Changing the fold changes which atoms can touch the target. Changing the target state changes which binder shape is useful. Changing the building blocks changes whether the candidate can be made at all. Changing the batch changes which candidate is selected, because experiments often care about the best found design, not the average design. Benchmark-neighbor checks matter because a test can look strong when the model is only copying a nearby known structure. The method is believable only when all these checks still point to the same candidate and the same claim.

a candidate is valid when sequence, structure, target context, making route, selection goal, and benchmark-neighbor boundary all support the same design claim

Evidence Check

Report target-specific binding, fold and assembled-structure validity, atom-level clash rates, transition-direction success, whether the candidate can be made, whether best-of-batch confidence matches success, benchmark-neighbor splits, fitness when tested away from familiar examples, and cases where sequence plausibility and lab success disagree.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when target context is underspecified, when synthetic pretraining creates binder artifacts, when best-of-batch selection overfits a substitute score, when makeability is checked too late, or when benchmarks include structural neighbors that make memorization look like design.

Connection

ReaSyn and MORetro* make route feasibility explicit; quotient diffusion removes irrelevant symmetry; scientific sampling preserves downstream scientific quantities. This group states the biology-level rule: a designed molecule or protein is a coupled object, not an isolated string.

EXEMPLAR 157

Synthetic Data Selection, Neon, FAC Synthesis, TabStruct, SurvDiff, PetaGAIL++, SceneSmith, TiTok, And COMPACT

Adding Fake Examples Only Where Real Evidence Is Missing

Problem

Fake practice problems help only if they cover what students have not seen. They hurt if they repeat easy cases, teach impossible situations, hide how observations were stopped, or reveal private routines. Synthetic data has the same rule: it must repair missing evidence for the real task.

State Space

The state is real data source, synthetic generator, how variables move together, an internal model signal that turns on for an important missing case, missing region, generator bias, observation-stopping rule, mobility path, privacy budget, physical scene constraint, and later estimator.

Object

The object is replacement training data that repairs missing evidence instead of merely increasing the row count. A student does not learn multiplication better by receiving the same easy problem a thousand times; the helpful new problem is the one that covers a missing case without teaching a false rule. Generated examples should be added where real evidence is missing, and they should keep the relationships the later task will use. Synthetic Data Selection targets directions of variation instead of matching only averages. Neon treats generator bias as evidence about regions the generator does not know well. FAC Synthesis generates examples for under-covered internal features. TabStruct preserves table relationships. SurvDiff preserves event times and the rule that says when observation stopped. PetaGAIL++ generates private mobility paths. SceneSmith builds physically valid robotics scenes. TiTok and COMPACT connect synthetic filtering and compact adaptation to transfer. The formal phrase is coverage repair: adding generated examples only where they fill a useful gap while preserving the task's real structure.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is downstream-valid coverage: synthetic examples should fill useful regions of the task data while preserving the structures the later estimator or action rule depends on.

Allowed Movement

What may change is generation, filtering, selection, privatization, diagnosis, or adaptation from synthetic examples. Each synthetic point must be judged by added value, structure preservation, and shift cost.

Why It Works

It works when synthetic data is treated as a deliberate change to the evidence the model receives. The question is not "Can we make more data?" The question is "Which missing situation would change what the later model can learn?" Relationships between variables matter because later analysis often uses how one quantity moves when another quantity changes, not just the average value of each column. Feature coverage matters because a model cannot learn behavior for an internal signal it never sees. Observation stopping matters because a survival label depends on both what happened and when watching ended. Privacy matters because a useful path through a city can reveal the person who took it. Physical scenes matter because a robot action rule learns contact, distance, and motion from the generated world. More synthetic volume helps only when the new examples add missing evidence without adding stronger false patterns.

add a synthetic sample when its coverage gain for the target task is larger than duplication, shift, privacy, and structure-violation costs

Evidence Check

Report coverage of variable relationships, internal signals the model needs to see often enough to learn the missing behavior, duplicate rates, real-versus-generated distance, later-estimator error, observation-stopping preservation, privacy exposure, physical simulation validity, what changes when selected synthetic data is replaced by random synthetic data, and cases where more synthetic volume lowers real performance.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when coverage is measured in the wrong feature space, when generated examples fill artifacts rather than missing modes, when privacy noise removes task signal, when physical constraints are incomplete, or when downstream gains come from benchmark contamination.

Connection

Sequential Data Values says value is conditional; Common Corpus says data needs source receipts; Local Redundancy asks whether the model can still use evidence. Synthetic-data papers combine these: generated data is useful only as coverage repair for a named task object.

EXEMPLAR 158

PhyWorldBench, AdAEM, WoW!, ImageDoctor, RealPDEBench, FLIP2, TEDBench/MiAE, RoboMME, And SAW-Bench

Testing The Rule That Would Make The Scientific Claim False

Problem

A science benchmark is weak if it only asks whether an output looks plausible. A video can look realistic while breaking physics. An image edit can look clean while violating the instruction. A protein benchmark can reward memorized neighbors instead of real generalization.

State Space

The state is generated artifact, physical rule, scientific system, benchmark item, domain constraint, evaluator, benchmark-neighbor cluster, action trace, edit instruction, environment transition, and observed violation.

Object

The object is a benchmark that watches the rule whose break would make the claim false. If a model claims to understand falling objects, the test has to watch motion over time, not just a nice-looking still frame. If a model claims to follow an image-edit instruction, the test has to check the requested change, not only whether the output is pretty. If a protein model claims new design ability, the test has to separate new cases from near-copies of known ones. PhyWorldBench checks whether generated videos obey physical principles. AdAEM and ImageDoctor evaluate artifact editing and repair under domain-specific constraints. WoW! asks whether extra planning compute improves closed-loop world-model behavior. RealPDEBench evaluates physical surrogates against real systems. FLIP2 and TEDBench/MiAE test protein generalization with controls for copied or near-copied examples. RoboMME and SAW-Bench measure embodied capability through environment-state transitions rather than language descriptions. The formal phrase is constraint-observation instrument: a test that can see the specific rule break that would invalidate the claim.

Preserved Quantity

What must stay the same is domain validity: the benchmark should observe the physical, scientific, or embodied constraint that defines success.

Allowed Movement

What may change is human review, domain models, benchmark-neighbor splits, action logs, repair metrics, physical simulations, or expert rules. The score must be tied to the constraint rather than to visual or textual plausibility alone.

Why It Works

It works by choosing the way of looking that can catch the relevant failure. Physics needs checks over time because a still frame can hide impossible motion. Protein evaluation needs neighbor-controlled splits because nearby structures can make memorized templates look like new design. Physical surrogate models need long rollouts and tests in different regimes because one accurate next step can still become unstable after many steps. Embodied benchmarks need state transitions because describing an action is not the same as changing the environment. Artifact repair needs instruction-specific checks because an edit can be visually clean while doing the wrong thing. Human review, domain models, simulations, action logs, and expert rules may all be useful, but the score is valid only when the chosen observation can see the violation that matters.

a domain benchmark is valid when the score observes a constraint whose satisfaction is necessary for the claimed capability

Evidence Check

Report physical-violation categories, action-state logs, long-rollout scientific error, benchmark-neighbor-controlled splits, expert disagreement, instruction-specific edit failures, closed-loop world-model outcomes, and examples where generic judges rank an invalid artifact highly.

Failure Boundary

The approach fails when constraints are underspecified, when human raters reward realism over physics, when benchmark-neighbor controls are incomplete, when simulations omit real friction or contact, when edits are judged by beauty rather than instruction, or when benchmark items can be solved from language priors.

Connection

ATLAS treats benchmarks as measurement instruments; IRNO needs structure-sensitive physical evaluation; WebDevJudge checks native artifacts. This group gives the domain rule: a benchmark is scientific only when it observes the constraint that would make the claim false.

Source: local conference math atlas. Added as worked exemplars for applying the first-principles paper template at full depth.
